Modal meet-implication logic
Jim de Groot, Dirk Pattinson

Abstract
. We extend the meet-implication fragment of propositional intuitionistic logic with a meet-preserving modality. We give semantics based on semilattices and a duality result with a suitable notion of descriptive frame. As a consequence we obtain completeness and identify a common (modal) fragment of a large class of modal intuitionistic logics. We recognise this logic as a dialgebraic logic, and as a consequence obtain expressivity-somewhere-else. Within the dialgebraic framework, we then investigate the extension of the meet-implication fragment of propositional intuitionistic logic with a monotone modality and prove completeness and expressivity-somewhere-else for it.



Dec. 29, 2020Jul. 14, 2022

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 18, Issue 3, Paper 1

1.
Introduction

In the field of mathematical logic, one does not only study logical formalisms,
but also the relationships between them.
The main reason for this is that one can often transfer results from one logic
to the other, like complexity or completeness results.

Two formalisms may differ because they have a different set of connectives.
For example, classical propositional logic contains connectives that are not
expressible in positive logic.
Nonetheless, the two logics are closely related:
classical propositional logic is a conservative extension of positive logic.
That is, a positive propositional formula is valid in positive logic
if and only if it is classically valid, when viewed as a formula of
classical propositional logic.
Similarly intuitionistic logic is a conservative
extension of its
(\top,\wedge,\to)
-fragment (that we call
\mathbf{MI}
)
and of its positive fragment.
Even if two logical formalisms use the same language,
they can still have different axioms or rules.
An example of this phenomenon can be found in the multitude of different modal extensions
of intuitionistic logic
[
Bul66
,
Fis80
,
BozDos84
,
Yok85
,
PloSti86
,
WolZak98
]
.

Another interesting instance is the comparison of positive
modal
logic
and classical modal logic. This traces back to Dunn
[
Dun95
]
,
whose paper on positive modal logic was motivated by finding an
axiomatisation for the positive fragment of normal modal logic.
As a consequence, the logic he found automatically has classical normal modal logic
as a conservative extension.
More recently, the monotone modal logic analogue hereof has been studied
[
Gro20
]
.

In analogy to this, we investigate the relation between modal extensions
of intuitionistic logic and modal extensions of
\mathbf{MI}
.
On first sight this may seem like a daunting task, considering the wide
variety of modal intuitionistic logics mentioned above.
However, it turns out that the
extension
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
of
\mathbf{MI}
with a meet-preserving
unary modality
{\boxempty}
is a common denominator of many of them,
thus making the task at hand much more manageable.
More precisely, we prove that the modal extensions of intuitionistic logic given by
Božić and Došen
[
BozDos84
]
, Plotkin and Sterling
[
PloSti86
]
,
Fisher Servi
[
Fis80
]
,
and Wolter and Zakharyaschev
[
WolZak98
]
are all conservative extensions of

\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.

In order to establish this, we carry out a semantic study of

\mathbf{MI}
and
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.
We begin by giving semantics for
\mathbf{MI}

based on meet-semilattices, that we call I-models.
In such I-models, formulae are interpreted as filters;
these form natural denotations of formulae because the collection of filters of
a meet-semilattice is closed under intersections but not
under unions.
We restrict the known duality between meet-semilattices and so-called
M-spaces from
[
DavWer83
]
to a duality for
implicative
semilattices.
We refer to those M-spaces dual to an implicative semilattice as I-spaces.
They form a category that is isomorphic to a category of descriptive frames.
(That this situation is completely analogous to the intuitionistic logic
framework becomes apparent when looking at Figure
.)

Subsequently, we enrich the language of
\mathbf{MI}
with a box-like
modal operator that preserves finite meets to obtain
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.
This modal operator is interpreted via its own accessibility relation
in what we call
{\boxempty}
-frames.
Based on this, we define
descriptive

{\boxempty}
-frames for
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.
These give rise to a duality for
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
which
piggy-backs on the duality for implicative semilattices.
As a consequence of this duality we not only derive weak completeness,
but we also observe that many famous modal
intuitionistic
logics
are conservative extensions of
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.

We then recognise the setup for
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
as an instance of a

dialgebraic logic
. The framework of dialgebraic logic is a slight
generalisation of coalgebraic logic and was recently proposed as a
suitable framework for the study of modal intuitionistic logics
[
GroPat20
]
.
After we detail how
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
fits the dialgebraic perspective,
we instantiate results from
op. ​cit.
to obtain the notion of
a
filter extension
of a frame for
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.
Furthermore, we obtain
expressivity-somewhere-else
:
two states in two models are logically equivalent if and only if
certain corresponding states in the filter extensions of the models
are behaviourally equivalent.

Once we have this dialgebraic perspective on modal extensions of

\mathbf{MI}
, we use it to sketch the extension
\mathbf{MI_{{\vartriangle}}}
of
\mathbf{MI}
with a
monotone modal operator
{\vartriangle}
. As a consequence of the dialgebraic approach,
we get weak completeness, filter extensions, and expressivity-somewhere-else.
Furthermore, with only minor additional effort one can see that
intuitionistic logic with a
geometric
modality,
see e.g.
[
Gol93
, Section 6]
, is a conservative extension of
\mathbf{MI_{{\vartriangle}}}
.

Structure of the paper

In Section
2
we recall basic facts about meet-semilattices,
a duality for meet-semilattices, and implicative semilattices.
Subsequently, in Section
3
we give a semantics for the
meet-implication fragment of intuitionistic logic that is based on meet-semilattices.
We also define the notion of a descriptive I-frame, show that these are
isomorphic to so-called I-spaces, which in turn are dually equivalent to
implicative semilattices.

In Section
we start the study of a normal modal extension
of
\mathbf{MI}
. We provide frame semantics by means of what we call
{\boxempty}
-frames,
and show how to view these as dialgebras.
Building on this, in Section
we define general and descriptive

{\boxempty}
-frames. We then show that these can again be viewed as dialgebras,
and we prove a duality with the algebraic semantics of
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.
In Section
we prove that many modal intuitionistic logics
are conservative extensions of
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
.

Subsequently, in Section
, we recall the framework of
dialgebraic logic and show that
\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}
is an instance
of this. As a consequence, we get a notion of filter extensions
and expressivity-somewhere-else.
In Section
we make use of this dialgebraic perspective to
investigate
\mathbf{MI}
with a monotone modality
{\vartriangle}
, and obtain similar results
as for
{\boxempty}
.

Related work

The meet-implication fragment of intuitionistic propositional logic has been studied by itself in

[
Cur63
, Section 4.C]
and more recently in e.g.
[
BezEA15
,
FonMor14
]
.
Its appeal partly lies in the fact that the category of meet-semilattices
is finitely generated, whereas the category of Heyting algebras is not.
Implicative meet-semilattices have also been studied by themselves,
see e.g.
[
Nem65
,
NemWha71
]
.
In
[
Mon55
]
a set of equational axioms for implicative meet-semilattices
is given, which proves that the category of implicative meet-semilattices
is a variety of algebras.

There are several different dualities for (implicative) meet-semilattices.
In
[
Bal69
]
, the author gives a representation theorem for prime semilattices and implicative
semilattices by means of prime filters.
A duality for meet-semilattices where the dual spaces are topologised meet-semilattices
was given by Davey and Werner in
[
DavWer83
]
.
More recently, in
[
BezJan11
]
and
[
BezJan13
]
a Priestley and Esakia-style duality for distributive
(implicative) meet-semilattices is developed.
We have opted for the restriction of the duality from
[
DavWer83
]

because it arises as a topologised version of implicative semilattices and,
as such, is intuitive to use as a notion of descriptive frame.

2.
Meet-semilattices

We recall basic definitions and facts that are either known or elementary.

Posets

Let
(X,\leq)
be a poset and
a\subseteq X
. We write

{\uparrow}a=\{x\in X\mid y\leq x\text{ for some }y\in a\}

and we call
a

up-closed
or an
upset
if
{\uparrow}a=a
.
Similarly define
{\downarrow}a
and
downsets
.
A
monotone function
from
(X,\leq)
to
(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
is a map

f:X\to X^{\prime}
such that
x\leq y
implies
f(x)\leq^{\prime}f(y)
.
It is called
bounded
or a
p-morphism
if moreover for all

x\in X
and
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
such that
f(x)\leq^{\prime}y^{\prime}
there exists

y\in X
such that
x\leq y
and
f(y)=y^{\prime}
.
We write
\mathsf{Pos}
and
\mathsf{Krip}
for the categories of posets with
monotone morphisms and bounded morphisms, respectively.

Meet-semilattices

A
meet-semilattice
is a poset in which every finite subset has
a greatest lower bound, called the
meet
.
The meet two elements
a
and
b
is denoted by

a\wedge b
, and the meet of the empty set is the top element
\top
.
A
meet-semilattice morphism
is a monotone
function that preserves finite meets (hence also
\top
).
We sometimes write
(X,\wedge,\top)
for the meet-semilattice corresponding
corresponding to a poset
(X,\leq)
.
We can recover
\leq
from
(X,\wedge,\top)
via
x\leq y
iff
x\wedge y=x
.

The category of meet-semilattices (with top)
and meet-semilattice morphisms is denoted by
\mathsf{SL}
.
It forms a variety of algebras, and the free
meet-semilattice on a set
X
is given by finite subsets of
X

(intuitively, these are finite meets), with
\wedge
given by union
and top element
\emptyset
.

Throughout this text, by
semilattice
we always mean
meet-semilattice with top.

The contravariant filter functor

A
filter
in a semilattice
A
is a non-empty up-closed subset that is
closed under finite meets.
A
prime down-set
is a down-closed subset
d
of
A

that satisfies: if
a\wedge a^{\prime}\in d
then
a\in d
or
a^{\prime}\in d
.
A subset
p\subseteq A
is a filter of
A
if and only if its complement

p^{c}=A\setminus p
is a prime downset.
Let
2
be the two-element semilattice and
p\subseteq A
.
Then the characteristic map
\chi_{p}:A\to 2
, which sends the elements of
p

to
1
and all other elements to
0
, is a semilattice morphism
if and only if
p
is a filter.

We write
\mathfun{F}A
be the collection of filters of a semilattice
A
.
With conjunction
\cap
and top element
A
, the set
\mathfun{F}A
forms a semilattice again.
We can extend this assignment to a contravariant functor
\mathfun{F}:\mathsf{SL}\to\mathsf{SL}

by defining the action of
\mathfun{F}
on a morphism
h:A\to A^{\prime}

by
\mathfun{F}h:\mathfun{F}A^{\prime}\to\mathfun{F}A:p^{\prime}\mapsto h^{-1}(p^{\prime})
.

For every
a\in A
the collection

\widetilde{a}=\{p\in\mathfun{F}A\mid a\in p\}
is a filter in
\mathfun{F}A
.
This gives rise to a morphism
\eta_{A}:A\to\mathfun{FF}A:a\mapsto\widetilde{a}
.
In fact, the collection

\eta=(\eta_{A})_{A\in\mathsf{SL}}:\id_{\mathsf{SL}}\to\mathfun{FF}
is a natural transformation.
To see this, suppose
h:A\to B
is a homomorphism, and let
a\in A

and
p\in\mathfun{FF}B
. Then we have

p\in(\mathfun{FF}h)(\eta_{A}(a))\quad\text{iff}\quad f^{-1}(p)\in\eta_{A}(a)\quad\text{iff}\quad a\in f^{-1}(p)\quad\text{iff}\quad f(a)\in p\quad\text{iff}\quad p\in\eta_{B}(f(a)).

Proposition 1
.

The functor
\mathfun{F}:\mathsf{SL}\to\mathsf{SL}
is dually adjoint to
itself. It defines a dual adjunction between
\mathsf{SL}
and itself
with units
\eta_{A}
.

Proof 2.1
.

We verify that
\mathfun{F}\eta_{A}\circ\eta_{\mathfun{F}A}=\id_{\mathfun{F}A}
.
Let
a\in A
and
p\in\mathfun{F}A
. Then we have

a\in\mathfun{F}\eta_{A}(\eta_{\mathfun{F}A}(p))\quad\text{iff}\quad\eta_{A}(a)\in\eta_{\mathfun{F}A}(p)\quad\text{iff}\quad p\in\eta_{A}(a)\quad\text{iff}\quad a\in p.

This completes the proof.

Duality for semilattices

We can adapt the dual adjunction to a dual equivalence by topologising one side of the
dual adjunction.
An
M-space
is a tuple

\mathbb{X}=(X,\wedge,\top,\tau)
such that
(X,\wedge,\top)
is a meet-semilattice,
and
(X,\tau)
is a Stone space generated by a subbase
of clopen filters and their complements. We write
\mathsf{MSpace}
for the category of M-spaces and
continuous semilattice homomorphisms.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathbb{X}=(X,\wedge,\top,\tau)
be an M-space
and
c\subseteq X
a filter in
(X,\wedge,\top)
.
Then
c
is closed in
(X,\tau)
if and only if
it is the intersection of all clopen filters that contain
c
.

Proof 2.2
.

The direction from right to left follows immediately from the fact that
the arbitrary intersection of clopen sets is closed.
For the converse, suppose
x\notin c
.
Then for each
y\in c
there exists a clopen filter
a_{y}

such that
y\in a_{y}
and
x\notin a_{y}
.
(To see this, note that
x\wedge y\leq y
. Since

(X,\tau)
is a Stone space there exists a clopen set
a
containing

y
and not
x\wedge y
. Since
(X,\tau)
is generated by clopen filters
and their complements, we may assume that
a
is either a clopen filter
or the complement of a clopen filter. But the latter is not an option,
as it would imply
x\wedge y\in a
, so
a
is a clopen filter.
Furthermore, we have
x\notin a
because
x\in a
would imply

x\wedge y\in a
, as
a
is a filter.)
Then we have an open cover
c\subseteq\bigcup\{a_{y}\mid y\in c\}
,
so there exists a finite subcover, say,
c\subseteq a_{1}\cup\cdots\cup a_{n}
.
Finally, we claim
c\subseteq a_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
.
For suppose this is not the case, then for each
a_{i}
we can find an element

z_{i}
such that
z_{i}\in c
and
z_{i}\notin a_{i}
. This implies

z_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge z_{n}\in c
, because
c
is a filter,
but
z_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge z_{n}
not in
a_{i}
for each
i
,
because that would imply
z_{i}\in a_{i}
, a contradiction.
So there must exist a clopen filter containing
c
. Moreover
this clopen filter does not contain
x
by construction.
It follows that
c
is the intersection of all clopen filters containing it.

Define
\mathfun{F^{t}}:\mathsf{SL}\to\mathsf{MSpace}
by sending a semilattice
A

to the semilattice of filters with a topology generated by

\widetilde{a}=\{p\in\mathfun{F}A\mid a\in p\},\qquad\widetilde{a}^{c}=\{p\in\mathfun{F}A\mid a\notin p\},

where
a
ranges over
A
. For a morphism
h
define
\mathfun{F^{t}}h=h^{-1}
.
Conversely, define
\mathfun{Clp^{f}}:\mathsf{MSpace}\to\mathsf{SL}
by sending an
M-space
\mathbb{X}
to its collection of clopen filters,
viewed as a semilattice with meet
\cap
and top
\mathbb{X}
.
For a continuous semilattice morphism
h
set
\mathfun{Clp^{f}}h=h^{-1}
.
The following result is originally due to Hofmann, Mislove and Stralka

[
HofMisStr74
]
, and can also be found in
[
DavWer83
]
.

Theorem 3
.

The functors
\mathfun{F^{t}}
and
\mathfun{Clp^{f}}
define a dual equivalence

\mathsf{MSpace}\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{SL}
.

Distributive semilattices

A meet-semilattice
A
is called
distributive
if for all

a,b,c\in A
, whenever
a\wedge b\leq c
there exist

a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
such that
a\leq a^{\prime},b\leq b^{\prime}
and
c=a^{\prime}\wedge b^{\prime}
.
Write
\mathsf{DSL}
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{SL}
with
distributive semilattices as objects.

In a distributive meet-semilattice
A
we can define the smallest filter
containing two given filters
p
and
q
by

\langle p,q\rangle=\{a\wedge b\mid a\in p,b\in q\}.

To see that this is again a filter, note that

\top\in\langle p,q\rangle
because
\top=\top\wedge\top

and
\top\in p,\top\in q
;
the set
\langle p,q\rangle
is closed under meets because

p
and
q
are, so

(a\wedge b)\wedge(a^{\prime}\wedge b^{\prime})=(a\wedge a^{\prime})\wedge(b\wedge b^{\prime})
;
and it is up-closed precisely by distributivity.

The following proposition also follows
from
[
Gra78
, Section II.5, Lemma 1]
.

Proposition 4
.

If
A
is a distributive semilattice, then so is
\mathfun{F}A
.

Proof 2.3
.

Let
p,q,r\in\mathfun{F}A
be such that
p\cap q\subseteq r
.
Let
p^{\prime}=\langle p,r\rangle
and
q^{\prime}=\langle q,r\rangle
.
We claim that
p^{\prime}\cap q^{\prime}=r
.
For the inclusion from right to left, observe

r=(p\cap q)\cup r=(p\cup r)\cap(q\cup r)\subseteq p^{\prime}\cap q^{\prime}.

For the converse we need to work a bit harder.
Suppose
x\in p^{\prime}\cap q^{\prime}
. Since
x\in p^{\prime}
we can write
x=y\wedge z

for some
y\in p
and
z\in r
.
Moreover,
x\leq y
(because
x\wedge y=(y\wedge z)\wedge y=y\wedge z=x
),
so
y\in p^{\prime}\cap q^{\prime}
. Using that
y\in q^{\prime}
we write
y=y^{\prime}\wedge z^{\prime}

for some
y^{\prime}\in q
and
z^{\prime}\in r
.
Since
y\in p
and
y\leq y^{\prime}
we must have
y^{\prime}\in p
, so that

y^{\prime}\in p\cap q
. In particular this means
y^{\prime}\in r
.
We now have
x=y\wedge z=y^{\prime}\wedge z^{\prime}\wedge z
,
where
y^{\prime},z^{\prime},z\in r
. This implies
x\in r
, and hence

p^{\prime}\cap q^{\prime}\subseteq r
.

Corollary 5
.

The dual adjunction between
\mathsf{SL}
and itself restricts to
a dual adjunction between
\mathsf{DSL}
and itself.

Implicative semilattices

A semilattice
A
is
implicative
if we can define a binary
operation
\xrightarrow{\circ}
such that
x\leq y\xrightarrow{\circ}z
iff
x\wedge y\leq z

for all
x,y,z\in A
.
An implicative semilattice homomorphism is a map that preserves
top, meets and implications.
We write
\mathsf{ISL}
for the category of implicative semilattice and
homomorphisms.

Proposition 6
.

If
A
is a distributive semilattice, then
\mathfun{F}A
is an implicative semilattice.

Proof 2.4
.

Let
a,b\in\mathfun{F}A
.
We prove that
C=\{x\in X\mid\text{ if }x\leq y\text{ and }y\in a\text{ then }y\in b\}
is again a filter.
Clearly it is up-closed and contains
\top
. Suppose
x,y\in C
and let

z
be such that
x\wedge y\leq z
and
z\in a
.
Since
A
is assumed to be distributive, we can find
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
such that

x\leq x^{\prime}
,
y\leq y^{\prime}
and
z=x^{\prime}\wedge y^{\prime}
.
We have
x^{\prime}\in a
, because
z\leq x^{\prime}

(indeed,
z\wedge x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\wedge y^{\prime}\wedge x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}\wedge y^{\prime}=z
)
and for the same reason
y^{\prime}\in a
. But then we must have
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in b
,
because
x\leq x^{\prime}
and
y\leq y^{\prime}
, and since
b
is a filter

z=x^{\prime}\wedge y^{\prime}\in b
. Therefore
C
is a filter of
A
,
so
C=a\xrightarrow{\circ}b
.

Since the semilattice underlying an implicative semilattice is
automatically distributive, this implies:

Corollary 7
.

If
A
is an implicative semilattice, then so is
\mathfun{F}A
.

3.
Frames for the meet-implication-fragment of intuitionistic logic

In this section we describe semantics and a duality for the base logic
underlying the modal logics discussed in this paper: the meet-implication
fragment of intuitionistic logic.
The language
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
of this fragment is given by the grammar

\varphi::=\top\mid p\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\to\varphi

where
p
ranges over
\Prop
, some set of proposition letters.
The algebraic semantics of the fragment are given by implicative semilattices.
They have been well-researched, see e.g.
[
Nem65
]
.
We give a Hilbert-style axiomatisation of this logic.

{defi}

The Hilbert-style deductive system for the meet-implication
fragment of intuitionistic propositional logic is given by the
axioms

(1)

p\to(q\to p)

(2)

(p\to(q\to r))\to((p\to q)\to(p\to r))

(3)

(p\wedge q)\to p

(4)

(p\wedge q)\to q

(5)

p\to(q\to(p\wedge q))

(6)

\top

If
\Gamma
is a set of formulae and
\varphi
is a formula, we
say that
\varphi

is deducible in
{H}
from
\Gamma

if
\Gamma\vdash_{H}\varphi
can be derived using the
rules

\mbox{(\assum)}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}\varphi}(\mbox{if } \varphi\in\Gamma)
\hskip 20.44434pt\hskip 10.22217pt\mbox{($\mathrm{ax}$)}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}\varphi} (\mbox{if } \varphi\in H)
\hskip 20.44434pt\hskip 10.22217pt\mbox{({mp})}\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{H}\varphi\hskip 20.44434pt\Gamma\vdash_{H}\varphi\to\psi}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}\psi}
$$
where $H$ is the set of substitution instances of the axioms
\ref{it:H1} to \ref{it:H6} above.
We write ${H}\vdash\varphi$ if $\emptyset\vdash_{H}\varphi$
and we let $\bf{MI}$ denote the collection of formulae $\varphi$ such that
${H}\vdash\varphi$.
\par It is proved in the appendix that this Hilbert style axiomatisation
indeed corresponds to the equational logic of implicative semilattices.
\par The fact that the meet-implication reduct of a Heyting algebra is an
implicative semilattice implies that any sound semantics for
intuitionistic logic also provides a sound semantics for $\mathbf{MI}$.
The best-known frame semantics of intuitionistic logic is given by posets,
often called intuitionistic Kripke frames.
So intuitionistic Kripke frames can be taken as frame semantics for $\mathbf{MI}$.
\par\par However, we will use a more restricted version of intuitionistic Kripke frames
as our semantics.
The main advantages of this restriction are that it lies closer to the duality
between frame semantics and implicative semilattices we intend to use.
Moreover, its completeness implies completeness with respect to intuitionistic
Kripke frames in a straightforward way.
We exploit the fact that the collection of filters of an implicative semilattice
is again an implicative semilattice and use these as frame semantics
for $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$-formulae.
Since every (implicative) semilattice carries an underlying partial order
given by $x\leq y$ iff $x\wedge y=x$, we can think of the frame semantics
as intuitionistic Kripke frames with extra properties.
When using them as frame semantics, we refer to implicative semilattices
as I-frames, both to distinguish them from the algebraic semantics,
and because morphisms between I-frames are not the same as implicative
semilattice homomorphisms.
\par\defi An \emph{M-frame} a semilattice viewed as intuitionistic Kripke frame.
That is, an M-frame is is a poset $(X,\leq)$ with meets.
We write $\mathsf{MF}$ for the category of M-frames and functions that
preserve finite meets. (Indeed, $\mathsf{MF}\cong\mathsf{SL}$.)
\par An \emph{I-frame} is an implicative semilattice regarded as a Kripke frame.
An I-frame morphism from $(X,\leq)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ is an $M$-frame
morphism $f$ such that $f(x)\leq^{\prime}y^{\prime}$ implies the existence of a $y\in X$
such that $x\leq y$ and $f(y)=y^{\prime}$.
We write $\mathsf{IF}$ for the category of I-frames and I-frame morphisms.
\par As announced, I-frames are of course simply implicative semilattices,
viewed as a special kind of intuitionistic Kripke frame.
Besides, note that an I-frame morphism is an M-frame morphism that
is bounded with respect to the underlying posets.
We now define I-models as I-frames with a valuation.
Rather than using upsets as denotations of formulae in our language, we use
\emph{filters} (i.e.~upsets closed under finite meets).
This is natural because the collection of filters
is closed under intersections, but not under unions.
\par\par\defi A \emph{valuation} for an I-frame $(X,\leq)$
is a function $V$ that assigns to each proposition letter $p$
a filter of $(X,\leq)$, and an \emph{I-model} is an I-frame together with a valuation.
An \emph{I-model morphism} from $(X,\leq,V)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},V^{\prime})$ is
an I-frame morphism $f:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ satisfying $V=f^{-1}\circ V^{\prime}$.
\par The interpretation of $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$-formulae in an I-model $\mathcal{M}=(X,\leq,V)$
is given recursively via
\@@amsalign
\par\par Let $\mathcal{M}=(X,\leq,V)$ be an I-model.
We write $\mathcal{M}\Vdash\varphi$ if $\mathcal{M},x\Vdash\varphi$
for all $x\in X$. For an I-frame $(X,\leq)$ we write
$(X,\leq)\Vdash\varphi$ if $\mathcal{M}\Vdash\varphi$ for every
I-model based $\mathcal{M}$ on $(X,\leq)$.
\par Clearly every I-model is also an intuitionistic Kripke model,
and formulae are interpreted in the same way as in intuitionistic Kripke models.
Furthermore, an I-model morphism is in particular a bounded morphism between
Kripke models. Since these preserve truth of intuitionistic formulae we have:
\par\begin{prop}Let $h:(X,\leq,V)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},V^{\prime})$ be an I-model morphism.
Then for all $x\in X$ and $\varphi\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$ we have
$$x\Vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad f(x)\Vdash\varphi.$$\end{prop}\par\par\par\par\par Towards a duality result between certain (general) I-frames and implicative semilattices,
we now define \emph{descriptive} I-frames and corresponding morphisms.
Apart from giving us completeness for $\mathbf{MI}$ with respect to I-frames,
the duality underlies dualities for modal extensions in subsequent sections.
\par\defi A \emph{descriptive I-frame} is a tuple $(X,\leq,A)$
consisting of an I-frame $(X,\leq)$ and a collection
$A\subseteq\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)$ of filters such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$A$ is closed under $\cap$ and $\xrightarrow{\circ}$, given by
\begin{equation}a\xrightarrow{\circ}b=\{x\in X\mid\text{for all }y\geq x,\text{ if }y\in a\text{ then }y\in b\};\end{equation}\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$A$ is differentiated: if $x\not\leq y$ in $(X,\leq)$ then there
exists $a\in A$ such that $x\in a$ and $y\notin a$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$(X,\leq,A)$ is compact: every cover of $X$
consisting of elements in $A$ and complements of elements in $A$
has a finite subcover.
\end{enumerate}A \emph{descriptive I-frame morphism} from $(X,\leq,A)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},A^{\prime})$
is an I-frame morphism $h:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ such that
$h^{-1}(a^{\prime})\in A$ for all $a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}$.
Write $\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax IF}$ for the category of descriptive I-frames and
descriptive I-frame morphisms.
\par Alternatively, we can view a descriptive I-frame as a special kind of M-space.
We shall use this in order to derive a duality between descriptive I-frames and implicative
semilattices:
First we define the subcategory $\mathsf{ISpace}$ of $\mathsf{MSpace}$ (Definition \ref{def:ISpace});
second we prove that the category of descriptive I-frames and its morphisms is
isomorphic to $\mathsf{ISpace}$ (Proposition \ref{prop:DIF-IS});
and finally we show that the duality between M-spaces and
semilattices from Theorem \ref{thm:msl-duality} restricts to a duality
between I-spaces and \emph{implicative} semilattices (Theorem \ref{thm:IS-ISL}).
Diagrammatically it looks as in the left diagram of Figure \ref{fig:int}.
To illustrate that the situation is completely analogous to that of intuitionistic logic,
we have depicted the isomorphisms and dualities for intuitionistic logics in the
diagram on the right, where the horizontal dualities in the right column are known as Priestley
duality and Esakia duality.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{1}}{Dualities for intuitionistic logic and its meet-implication fragment.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{Figure 1}}{Dualities for intuitionistic logic and its meet-implication fragment.}}}
\end{figure}\par\defi An \emph{I-space} is an M-space whose collection of clopen filters is
closed under the operation $\xrightarrow{\circ}$ from Equation \eqref{eq:mto}.
An I-space morphism from $\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{Y}$ is a bounded M-space morphisms.
We write $\mathsf{ISpace}$ for the category of I-spaces and I-space morphisms.
\par\begin{prop}The category $\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax IF}$ is isomorphic to $\mathsf{ISpace}$.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}We only give the constructions.
If $\mathbb{X}$ is an I-space with underlying set $X$ we can define an order
$\leq$ on $X$ by $x\leq y$ if $x\wedge y=x$.
Then the tuple $(X,\leq,\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$ is a descriptive I-frame.
\par Conversely, for a descriptive I-frame $(X,\leq,A)$ we already know that
$(X,\leq)$ is a poset with a top element and binary meets, because it is
an I-frame, so we have a semilattice $(X,\wedge,\top)$.
Generate a topology $\tau_{A}$ from the subbase $A\cup-A$, where
$-A=\{X\setminus a\mid a\in A\}$. Then $\mathbb{X}=(X,\wedge,\top,\tau_{A})$
is an I-space.
\end{proof}\par\par Proposition \ref{prop:DIF-IS} allows us to use the notions of
a descriptive I-frame and an I-space interchangeably,
and henceforth we will do so.
We now prove that the category if I-spaces is dually equivalent to
the category of implicative semilattices. This ultimately proves that
descriptive I-frames are dual to implicative semilattices.
\par\begin{thm}We have a dual equivalence $\mathsf{ISpace}\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{ISL}$.
\end{thm}\begin{proof}Let $\mathbb{X}$ be an I-space.
Since in particular it is an M-space we know from Theorem \ref{thm:msl-duality}
that $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}$ is a semilattice with meets given by intersections.
It is easy to show that $\xrightarrow{\circ}$, defined as in~\eqref{eq:mto}, is residuated
with respect to $\cap$ in $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}$, so in fact the latter is implicative.
In the converse direction we have:
\par\clm If $A$ is an implicative semilattice, then $\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ is an $I$-space.
\begin{proof}By Theorem \ref{thm:msl-duality} $\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ is an M-space
and it follows from Corollary \ref{cor:imp-imp} that the semilattice
underlying $\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ is implicative, hence an I-frame. So we are left to show that
the clopen filters are closed under $\xrightarrow{\circ}$.
We do this by proving that $\widetilde{a}\xrightarrow{\circ}\widetilde{b}=\widetilde{a\to b}$
for all clopen filters $\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}(\mathfun{F^{t}}A)$,
where $\to$ is the implication from $A$ and $\xrightarrow{\circ}$ is defined on
the filters of $\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ as in~\eqref{eq:mto}.
\par Suppose $p\in\widetilde{a\to b}$ and $p\subseteq q$,
where $p,q\in\mathfun{F^{t}}A$. If $q\in\widetilde{a}$, then
we have $a\to b\in q$ and $a\in q$, hence
$(a\to b)\wedge a\in q$. Since $(a\to b)\wedge a\leq b$ this
implies $b\in q$, so $q\in\widetilde{b}$.
Therefore we have $p\in\widetilde{a}\xrightarrow{\circ}\widetilde{b}$.
\par Conversely, suppose $p\notin\widetilde{a\to b}$.
Then $a\to b\notin p$ and $b\notin p$ because $b\leq a\to b$.
We construct a filter $q\supseteq p$ such that $a\in q$ and $b\notin q$,
thus witnessing $p\notin({\downarrow}(\widetilde{a}\cap\widetilde{b}^{c}))^{c}=\widetilde{a}\xrightarrow{\circ}\widetilde{b}$.
Define $q={\uparrow}\{d\wedge a\mid d\in p\}$.
This is up-closed by definition and one can easily see that $q$ is closed
under finite meets, so $q$ is a filter. Suppose towards a contradiction that
$a\to b\in q$, then there is $d\in p$ such that
$d\wedge a\leq a\to b$, so $d\wedge a=d\wedge a\wedge a\leq b$
so $d\leq a\to b$. This implies $a\to b\in p$, which is a contradiction.
So $p\notin\widetilde{a\to b}$.
\end{proof}\par So we have now established the duality on objects.
We proceed to show that the restriction of $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}$ to the morphisms of $\mathsf{ISpace}$
lands in $\mathsf{ISL}$.
\par\clm Let $f:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}$ be an I-space morphism.
Then $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}f:\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}$ is an
implicative semilattice homomorphism.
\begin{proof}We already know that $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}f$ is a semilattice morphism
because of the dual equivalence between $\mathsf{SL}$ and $\mathsf{MSpace}$,
so we only need to show that it preserves implications.
Let $a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}$. We show that
$f^{-1}(a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime})=f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\circ}f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$.
\par Suppose $x\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime})$. Let $y$ be any successor of $x$.
If $y\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime})$ then $f(y)\in a^{\prime}$.
Since $f(x)\in a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime}$ and $f(x)\leq^{\prime}f(y)$ this implies $f(y)\in b^{\prime}$, so
$y\in f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$. Thus every successor $y$ of $x$ satisfies
$y\notin f^{-1}(a^{\prime})$ or $y\in f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$, that is,
$x\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\circ}f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$.
\par Conversely, suppose $x\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\circ}f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$.
We aim to show that $f(x)\in a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime}$.
Suppose $f(x)\leq^{\prime}y^{\prime}$ and $y^{\prime}\in a^{\prime}$.
Then since $f$ is bounded there
is $y\in X$ such that $x\leq y$ and $f(y)=y^{\prime}$. By assumption
$y\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime})$ hence $y\in f^{-1}(b^{\prime})$. This implies
$y^{\prime}=f(y)\in b^{\prime}$, so that $f(x)\in a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime}$, i.e.~$x\in f^{-1}(a^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\circ}b^{\prime})$.
\end{proof}\par Conversely, applying $\mathfun{F^{t}}$ to a semilattice homomorphism $h$
yields a continuous semilattice homomorphism.
We show that if moreover $h$ preserves implication,
then $\mathfun{F^{t}}h$ is bounded.
The proof is a basic adaptation of a similar proof for
Esakia spaces and Heyting algebras.
\par We make use of the following facts: If $A$ is a meet-semilattice then
the space $\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ has a basis of clopens of the form
$\widetilde{a}\cap\widetilde{b}_{1}^{c}\cap\cdots\cap\widetilde{b}_{m}^{c}$,
where $m$ is a non-negative integer and $a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\in A$.
Furthermore, for any filter $p\in\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ we have
$$\{p\}=\bigcap\{D\mid D\text{ is a basic clopen and }p\in D\}.$$\par\clm Let $h:A\to A^{\prime}$ be an implicative semilattice morphism.
Then $\mathfun{F^{t}}h:\mathfun{F^{t}}A^{\prime}\to\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ is bounded.
\begin{proof}Let $q^{\prime}\in\mathfun{F^{t}}A^{\prime}$ and $p\in\mathfun{F^{t}}A$ be such that
$\mathfun{F^{t}}h(q^{\prime})\subseteq p$.
Let $C=\widetilde{a}\cap\widetilde{b}_{1}^{c}\cap\cdots\cap\widetilde{b}_{m}^{c}$
be a basic clopen containing $p$. Then
\@@amsalign
Since $\mathfun{F^{t}}h(q^{\prime})\subseteq p$ we have
$q^{\prime}\in(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}({\downarrow}C)={\downarrow}((\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(C))$
for every basic clopen $C$ containing $p$.
This implies ${\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(C)\neq\emptyset$.
\par Since the finite intersection of basic clopens containing $p$ is nonempty,
compactness implies that
$\bigcap\{{\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(C)\}\neq\emptyset$,
where $C$ ranges over the basic clopens containing $p$.
Since the intersection of basic clopens containing $p$ is $\{p\}$
we have
\begin{equation*}\begin{split}\emptyset\neq\bigcap\big\{{\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(C)\big\}&={\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap\bigcap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(C)\\
&={\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}\Big(\bigcap C\Big)={\uparrow}q^{\prime}\cap(\mathfun{F^{t}}h)^{-1}(\{p\})\end{split}\end{equation*}so there must exist $p^{\prime}\in\mathfun{F}A^{\prime}$ such that $q^{\prime}\subseteq p^{\prime}$ and
$\mathfun{F^{t}}h(p^{\prime})=p$. This proves that $\mathfun{F^{t}}h$ is bounded.
\end{proof}\par In conclusion, the duality from Theorem \ref{thm:msl-duality} restricts to a
duality between $\mathsf{ISL}$ and $\mathsf{MSpace}$.
\end{proof}\par Given that $\mathbf{MI}$ is sound and complete with respect to implicative
semilattices by construction, this theorem implies completeness with
respect to descriptive I-frames, where propositional variables are
interpreted as admissible filters.
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{MI}$ is complete with respect to the classes of
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@descriptive I-frames;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@I-frames;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@intuitionistic Kripke frames.
\end{enumerate}\end{thm}\begin{proof}For the first item,
if $\mathbf{MI}\not\vdash\varphi$ then there is an algebraic model (i.e.~an
implicative semilattice with a valuation) where $\varphi$ does not evaluate to $\top$.
But this implies that the dual I-space model does not satisfy $\varphi$.
\par The second and third item follow since, in particular,
I-space models are I-models, and I-models are
intuitionistic Kripke models
\end{proof}\par Finally, we note that $\mathbf{MI}$ is \emph{not} the same as the $(\top,\wedge,\to)$-fragment
of classical propositional logic. This is witnessed by the fact that
Peirce's formula
$$((p\to q)\to p)\to p$$is valid classically but not intuitionistically. Therefore, as a consequence
of Theorem \ref{thm:MI-complete} it is not valid in $\mathbf{MI}$.
\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{A normal modal extension}
\par We enrich the meet-implication fragment $\mathbf{MI}$ of intuitionistic logic
with a modal operator ${\boxempty}$ satisfying
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@${\boxempty}(p\wedge q)\leftrightarrow{\boxempty}p\wedge{\boxempty}q$; and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@${\boxempty}\top\leftrightarrow\top$.
\end{enumerate}Write $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{{\boxempty}}$ for the extension of $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$ with unary operator ${\boxempty}$,
and $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ for the extension of (the derivation system giving) $\mathbf{MI}$ with the
axioms \ref{ax:box1} and \ref{ax:box2} and the congruence rule
$$\mbox{(cong)}\frac{p\leftrightarrow q}{{\boxempty}p\leftrightarrow{\boxempty}q}.$$The algebras corresponding to $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$
are given by \emph{implicative semilattices with operators} (ISLOs).
These are pairs $(A,{\boxempty})$ consisting of an implicative semilattice $A$ and a
meet-preserving function ${\boxempty}:A\to A$.
A homomorphism between ISLOs $(A,{\boxempty})$ and $(A^{\prime},{\boxempty}^{\prime})$ is an implicative
semilattice homomorphism $h:A\to A^{\prime}$ that additionally satisfies
$h\circ{\boxempty}={\boxempty}^{\prime}\circ h$.
Let $\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}$ denote the category of ISLOs and their morphisms.
\par The main goal of this section is twofold: First, we give relational semantics
for $\mathsf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$, in the form of I-frames with an additional relation.
We turn such frames into a category by defining an appropriate notion of
morphism, which we show to be truth-preserving. Furthermore, we prove that
these relational structures provide a sound semantics for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
Second, we show that both our category of relational frames, as well as
the category $\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}$, are categories of \emph{dialgebras}.
This realisation plays a key r\^{o}le in proving a duality result
for $\mathsf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ in Section \ref{sec:duality}, which in turn
underlies the completeness result for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ with respect to our
frame semantics.
\par Let us begin by defining ${\boxempty}$-frames.
\par\defi A ${\boxempty}$-frame is a tuple $(X,\leq,R)$ where $(X,\leq)$ is an I-frame
and $R$ is a binary relation on $X$ satisfying:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\top Rx$ iff $x=\top$, and $xR\top$ for all $x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $xRy\leq z$ then $xRz$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $xRy$ and $x^{\prime}Ry^{\prime}$ then $(x\wedge x^{\prime})R(y\wedge y^{\prime})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $(x\wedge x^{\prime})Rz$ then there are $y,y^{\prime}\in X$ such that
$xRy$ and $x^{\prime}Ry^{\prime}$ and $y\wedge y^{\prime}=z$.
\end{enumerate}A ${\boxempty}$-model is a ${\boxempty}$-frame together with a valuation $V:\Prop\to\mathfun{F}A$
that assigns a filter to each proposition letter.
The interpretation of a formula $\varphi$ in a ${\boxempty}$-model $\mathcal{M}=(X,\leq,R,V)$
is given recursively by the clauses of Definition \ref{def:I-model} and
$$\mathcal{M},x\Vdash{\boxempty}\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad xRy\text{ implies }\mathcal{M},y\Vdash\varphi.$$\par\defi A \emph{${\boxempty}$-frame morphism} from $(X,\leq,R)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},R^{\prime})$
is an I-frame morphism $h:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ between the
underlying I-frames such that $h:(X,R)\to(X^{\prime},R^{\prime})$ is a bounded morphism,
i.e.~$xRy$ implies $h(x)R^{\prime}h(y)$ and for all $x\in X$ and $y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}$, if $f(x)R^{\prime}y^{\prime}$ then there exists
$y\in X$ such that $xRy$ and $f(y)=y^{\prime}$.
A \emph{${\boxempty}$-model morphism} is a ${\boxempty}$-frame morphism that is also
an I-model morphism between the underlying I-models.
\par We denote the category of ${\boxempty}$-frames and ${\boxempty}$-frame morphisms
by $\mathsf{IF_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\par An easy induction on the structure of a formula shows that:
\par\begin{prop}If $h:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}$ is a ${\boxempty}$-model morphism,
$x$ is a world in $\mathcal{M}$ and $\varphi$ is an $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$-morphism,
then
$$\mathcal{M},x\Vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathcal{M}^{\prime},h(x)\Vdash\varphi.$$\end{prop}\par\begin{prop}If $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}\vdash\varphi$ then $\mathsf{IF_{{\boxempty}}}\Vdash\varphi$.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}We need to prove that every frame satisfies
${\boxempty}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\leftrightarrow{\boxempty}\varphi\wedge{\boxempty}\psi$ and
${\boxempty}\top\leftrightarrow\top$. Both follow immediately from the definition.
\end{proof}\par We can view both the category $\mathsf{IF_{{\boxempty}}}$ of ${\boxempty}$-frames as well as
the category $\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}$ of implicative semilattices with modal operators
as categories of dialgebras. Dialgebras generalise both algebras and coalgebras,
and were introduced by Hagino in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Hag87}{}{}]} to describe data types.
They were subsequently investigated in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{PolZwa01,AltEA11,Blo12,Cia13}{}{}]}.
We recall their definition:
\par\defi Let $\mathfun{G},\mathfun{H}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}$ be two functors.
A $(\mathfun{G},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebra is a pair $(X,\gamma)$ consisting of
an object $X\in\mathsf{C}$ and a morphism $\gamma:\mathfun{G}X\to\mathfun{H}X$ in $\mathsf{D}$.
A dialgebra morphism from $(X,\gamma)$ to $(X^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})$ is a morphism
$f:X\to X^{\prime}$ in $\mathsf{C}$ such that $\mathfun{H}f\circ\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\circ\mathfun{G}f$.
The collection of $(\mathfun{G},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebras and morphisms constitutes
the category $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{G},\mathfun{H})$.
\par It is fairly straightforward to model ISLOs as dialgebras.
Let $\mathfun{N}:\mathsf{ISL}\to\mathsf{SL}$ be the functor that sends an
implicative meet-semilattice $A$ to the free meet-semilattice generated by
$\{{\boxempty}a\mid a\in A\}$ modulo ${\boxempty}\top=\top$ and
${\boxempty}a\wedge{\boxempty}b={\boxempty}(a\wedge b)$,
and a homomorphism $h:A\to A^{\prime}$ to $\mathfun{N}h:\mathfun{N}A\to\mathfun{N}A^{\prime}$
generated by $\mathfun{N}h({\boxempty}a)={\boxempty}h(a)$. Then we have:
\par\begin{prop}Let $\mathfun{j}:\mathsf{ISL}\to\mathsf{SL}$ be the inclusion functor,
then
$$\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j}).$$\end{prop}\par We leave the obvious proof of this proposition to the reader.
Observe that $\mathfun{N}$ is naturally isomorphic to $\mathfun{j}$,
so that $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{j},\mathfun{j})$.
We shall exploit this fact in Section \ref{sec:duality}
to obtain a duality between ISLOs and descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames.
\par Modelling ${\boxempty}$-frames as dialgebras requires a bit more work.
Crucially, this uses the inclusion functor $\mathfun{i}:\mathsf{IF}\to\mathsf{MF}$
and the \emph{covariant filter functor}
$\mathfun{G}:\mathsf{IF}\to\mathsf{MF}$. This functor acts the same on objects as
the functor $\mathfun{F}$, but differs in its action on morphisms.
(Think of the co- and contravariant powerset functors in coalgebraic logic over
a classical base, where the covariant powerset functor gives rise to
Kripke frames~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Mos99}{}{}]}, and the covariant powerset functor takes a set to its
algebra of predicates.)
We define this functor and prove that it is well defined.
Subsequently, in Theorem \ref{thm:iG-dialg} we show that the category of ${\boxempty}$-frames
is isomorphic to $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$.
\par\defi Let $(X,\leq)$ be an I-frame. Define $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$ to be the collection
of filters ordered by reverse inclusion.
This is a meet-semilattice with top element $\{\top\}$ and meet defined by
$$a\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}b:=\langle a,b\rangle=\{x\wedge y\mid x\in a,y\in b\}.$$For an $\mathsf{IF}$-morphism $h:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ define
$$\mathfun{G}h:\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{G}(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime}):a\mapsto h[a]=\{h(x)\mid x\in a\}.$$\par Note that $a\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}b$ was shown to be a filter in Section \ref{sec:sl}.
In fact, it is the smallest filter containing both $a$ and $b$,
so alternatively we can define
$a\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}b=\bigcap\{c\in\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)\mid a\cup b\subseteq c\}$.
Thus $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$ has binary meets and a top element.
Hence $\mathfun{G}$ is well defined on objects. We show that the same
is true for morphisms.
\par\begin{lem}If $h$ is a $\mathsf{IF}$-morphism from $(X,\leq)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$,
then $\mathfun{G}h$ is an $\mathsf{MF}$-morphism.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}Let $a\in\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$.
Since $h$ is bounded the set $h[a]$ is up-closed in $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$.
Moreover, it contains $\top^{\prime}$ and is closed under $\wedge^{\prime}$
because $h$ preserves $\top$ and meets.
One easily sees that $\mathfun{G}h(\{\top\})=\{\top^{\prime}\}$.
Furthermore, for $a,b\in\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$ we have
\@@amsalign
So $\mathfun{G}h$ preserves the top element and binary meets, hence all finite meets.
\end{proof}\par A straightforward verification shows that $\mathfun{G}$ is indeed
a functor. We proceed to the main theorem of this section.
\par\begin{thm}Let $\mathfun{i}:\mathsf{IF}\to\mathsf{MF}$ be the inclusion functor.
Then $\mathsf{IF_{{\boxempty}}}\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$.
\end{thm}\par\begin{proof}We split the proof into three claims. The first two describe the isomorphism
on objects, and the last one proves the isomorphism for morphisms.
\par\clm Let $(X,\leq,R)$ be a ${\boxempty}$-frame and define
$\gamma_{R}:\mathfun{i}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{G}(X,\leq):x\mapsto R[x]$,
where $R[x]:=\{y\in X\mid xRy\}$.
Then $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra.
\begin{proof}For all $x\in X$ the set $\gamma_{R}(x)$ is a filter by definition of $R$.
We verify that $\gamma_{R}$ preserves meets and top.
By \ref{it:box-frm1} we have $\gamma_{R}(\top)=\{\top\}$, which is the top element in
$\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$.
It follows immediately from \ref{it:box-frm3} and \ref{it:box-frm4} that
$\gamma_{R}(x)\wedge_{\mathfun{G}}\gamma_{R}(y)=\gamma_{R}(x\wedge y)$.
\end{proof}\par\clm Let $(X,\leq)$ be an I-frame and $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ a
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra. Define $R_{\gamma}$ by
$xR_{\gamma}y$ iff $y\in\gamma(x)$. Then $(X,\leq,R)$ is a ${\boxempty}$-frame.
\begin{proof}We need to verify that $R_{\gamma}$ satisfies the four items
from Definition \ref{def:box-frm}.
Since $\gamma(\top)=\{\top\}$ \ref{it:box-frm1} holds.
By definition $\gamma(x)$ is a filter in $(X,\leq)$, hence up-closed.
This proves \ref{it:box-frm2}.
Items \ref{it:box-frm3} and \ref{it:box-frm4} follow from the fact
that $\gamma$ preserves meets.
\end{proof}\par It is straightforward to see that Claims \ref{clm:iG-dialg1} and \ref{clm:iG-dialg2}
establish an isomorphism on objects.
We complete the proof of the theorem by showing that the ${\boxempty}$-frame morphisms between two ${\boxempty}$-frames
are precisely the $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra morphisms between the corresponding
dialgebras.
Let $(X,\leq,R)$ and $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},R^{\prime})$ be two ${\boxempty}$-frames
with corresponding $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebras $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ and
$(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})$.
\par\clm An I-frame morphism $f:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ is a
${\boxempty}$-frame morphism from $(X,\leq,R)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},R^{\prime})$
if and only if it is an $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra morphism
from $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ to $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})$.
\begin{proof}Suppose $f$ is a ${\boxempty}$-frame morphism. In order to prove that it is also
a dialgebra morphism we need to show that
$$\hbox to141.65pt{\vbox to55.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.82616pt\lower-26.53725pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.53838pt}{-14.16365pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$commutes. To see this, let $x\in X$ and $y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}$. Then
\@@amsalign
Conversely, suppose $f$ is a dialgebra morphism. Then $f$ is monotone with respect
to $R$ and $R^{\prime}$ because $xRy$ implies $y\in\gamma(x)$ hence
$f(y)\in\mathfun{G}f(\gamma(x))=\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfun{i}f(x))$ so $f(x)R^{\prime}f(y)$.
For boundedness, suppose $f(x)R^{\prime}y^{\prime}$, then $y^{\prime}\in\gamma^{\prime}(\mathfun{i}f(x))=\mathfun{G}f(\gamma(x))$,
and this implies $f(y)=y^{\prime}$ for some $y\in\gamma(x)$, i.e.~for some $y$ with $xRy$.
\end{proof}\par Combining these three claims proves the theorem.
\end{proof}\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Descriptive \texorpdfstring{$\Box$}{box}-frames and duality}
\par Towards a duality for ISLOs
we now define \emph{descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames}.
We then show that these are dialgebras for the functors
$\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V}:\mathsf{ISpace}\to\mathsf{MSpace}$, where $\mathfun{I}$ is the inclusion
functor and $\mathfun{V}$ is a variation of the Vietoris functor.
Subsequently, we prove that $\mathfun{V}$ is naturally isomorphic to $\mathfun{I}$,
and as a consequence we obtain a duality between descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames
and implicative meet-semilattices with normal operators as follows:
$$\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{I})\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{j},\mathfun{j})\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j}).$$As a corollary we get completeness of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ with respect to
${\boxempty}$-frames.
\par\defi A \emph{general ${\boxempty}$-frame} is a tuple $(X,\leq,R,A)$ such that
$(X,\leq,R)$ is a ${\boxempty}$-frame, $(X,\leq,A)$ is a descriptive I-frame,
and $A$ is closed under
$$m_{{\boxempty}}:\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{F}(X,\leq):p\mapsto\{x\in X\mid R[x]\subseteq p\}.$$(Recall that we write $R[x]:=\{y\in X\mid xRy\}$.)
It is called \emph{descriptive} if moreover
$R[x]=\bigcap\{a\in A\mid R[x]\subseteq a\}$ for all $x\in X$.
\par A morphism between general ${\boxempty}$-frames is a function which is
simultaneously a ${\boxempty}$-frame morphism between the underlying ${\boxempty}$-frames,
and a descriptive I-frame morphism between the underlying descriptive I-frames.
The collection of descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames and general ${\boxempty}$-frame morphisms
forms a category, and since we tend to think of these as I-spaces
with a collection of admissibles we will denote it by $\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\par As stated the category $\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}$ can be described as a category of
dialgebras. We first define the relevant functor for doing so.
\par\defi Let $\mathbb{X}=(X,\leq,\tau)$ be an I-space. Call a filter
$c\in\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)$ \emph{closed} if it is the intersection of all
clopen filters in which it is contained. (By Lemma~\ref{lem:closed-clp-fil} it is equivalent to require that
$c$ is closed in $(X,\tau)$.)
Define $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ to be the collection of closed filters of $\mathbb{X}$ ordered by reverse inclusion and
topologised by the subbase
$${\boxdot}a=\{c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\mid c\subseteq a\},\qquad{\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{$\boxdot$}}}b=\{c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\mid c\cap b\neq\emptyset\},$$where $a$ ranges over the clopen filters of $\mathbb{X}$ and
$b$ over the clopen prime downsets of $\mathbb{X}$.
For an I-space morphism $h:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}$
define $\mathfun{V}h=h[-]$, i.e.~$\mathfun{V}h$ sends a closed filter $p$
to the direct image $h[p]$ of $p$ under $h$.
\par Note that the top element of $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ is $\{\top\}$
and conjunction of closed filters is given by
$$c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c^{\prime}=\bigcap\{a\in A\mid c\cup c^{\prime}\subseteq a\}.$$Before proving that descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames are $(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$-dialgebras,
we will prove that $\mathfun{V}$ is naturally isomorphic to $\mathfun{I}$.
This entails the useful fact that $c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c^{\prime}=\{y\wedge z\mid y\in c,z\in c^{\prime}\}$,
which we can then use to prove $\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$.
\par\begin{lem}For every I-space $\mathbb{X}$ the M-space $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ is isomorphic
to $\mathbb{X}$ in $\mathsf{MSpace}$. \end{lem}\begin{proof}Since $\mathfun{F^{t}}$ and $\mathfun{Clp^{f}}$ constitute a duality, it suffices
to prove that $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ is isomorphic to $\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$.
To achieve this, we define two continuous semilattice homomorphisms
and show that these are each other's inverses.
\par Define $f:\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$ by
$f(c)=\{a\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}(\mathbb{X})\mid c\subseteq a\}$. It is easy to verify that
this is a filter. Clearly $f(\{\top\})=\mathfun{Clp^{f}}(\mathbb{X})$, which is the
top element of $\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$, and
\@@amsalign
so $f$ preserves meets. Furthermore,
$$f^{-1}(\widetilde{a})=\{c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\mid f(c)\in\widetilde{a}\}=\{c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\mid a\in f(c)\}=\{c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\mid c\subseteq a\}={\boxdot}a$$so $f$ is continuous.
\par In the converse direction, define
$g:\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})\to\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}:p\mapsto\bigcap\{a\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}\mid a\in p\}$.
This is well defined because $g(p)$ is the intersection of filters in $A$.
Furthermore, $g(p)\subseteq a$ iff $a\in p$.
(Proof: if $g(p)\subseteq a$ then there are (potentially infinite) $b_{i}\in A$
such that $b_{i}\in p$ and $\bigcap b_{i}\subseteq a$. By compactness
we can find a finite number of such $b_{i}$, say, $b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}$,
such that $b_{1}\cap\cdots\cap b_{m}\subseteq a$. But then
$b_{1}\cap\cdots\cap b_{m}\in p$ because $p$ is a filter,
hence $a\in p$ as $p$ is up-closed.)
Moreover, $g(A)=\{\top\}$ and
\@@amsalign
so $g$ preserves top and binary meets, hence all finite meets.
Finally,
$$g^{-1}({\boxdot}a)=\{p\mid g(p)\subseteq a\}=\{p\mid a\in p\}=\widetilde{a}.$$proves continuity. Clearly $f$ and $g$ are inverses, and hence
$\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}\cong\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$.
\end{proof}\par Since we know that $\mathbb{X}$ is isomorphic to $\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$
via the map $x\mapsto\widetilde{x}=\{a\in A\mid x\in a\}$,
it follows that every $c\in\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ is of the form ${\uparrow}x$
for some $x\in\mathbb{X}$.
As a consequence, the meet of $c$ and $c^{\prime}$ in $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$
is
$$c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c^{\prime}=\{y\wedge z\mid y\in c,z\in c^{\prime}\}.$$To see this, write $c={\uparrow}x_{c}$ and $c^{\prime}={\uparrow}x_{c^{\prime}}$.
Then $c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c^{\prime}$ in $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ corresponds to
$\widetilde{x}_{c}\cap\widetilde{x}_{c^{\prime}}=\widetilde{x_{c}\wedge x_{c^{\prime}}}$
in $\mathfun{F^{t}}(\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X})$, so $c\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c^{\prime}={\uparrow}(x_{c}\wedge x_{c^{\prime}})$.
\par\begin{lem}The functors $\mathfun{V}$ and $\mathfun{F^{t}}\cdot\mathfun{Clp^{f}}$ are naturally isomorphic.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}Define the transformation
$(f_{\mathbb{X}})_{\mathbb{X}\in\mathsf{ISpace}}:\mathfun{V}\to\mathfun{F^{t}}\cdot\mathfun{Clp^{f}}$
on objects as in Lemma \ref{lem:V-iso}. We show that it is natural.
Since by Lemma \ref{lem:V-iso} it is isomorphic on objects, this proves
the claimed natural isomorphism.
\par Suppose $h:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}$ is an I-space morphism.
We need to show that
$$\hbox to135.01pt{\vbox to56.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.08249pt\lower-27.50945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-63.08249pt}{-21.34972pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

$a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}$, and compute
\@@amsalign
This proves the proposition.
\end{proof}\par We now prove that descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames are indeed $(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$-dialgebras.
\par\begin{thm}We have $\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$.
\end{thm}\begin{proof}We first establish the isomorphism on objects.
\par\clm For a descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frame $(X,\leq,R,A)$, let
$\mathbb{X}$ be the I-space isomorphic to the descriptive I-frame $(X,\leq,A)$
and define
$$\gamma_{R}:\mathfun{I}\mathbb{X}\to\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}:x\mapsto R[x].$$Then $(\mathbb{X},\gamma_{R})$ is a $(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$-dialgebra.
\begin{proof}We need to show that $\gamma_{R}$ is an M-space morphism from $\mathbb{X}$ to
$\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$. It is well defined since the fact that $(X,\leq,R,A)$ is
descriptive implies that $\gamma_{R}(x)=R[x]$ is a closed filter for each $x$.
Besides, $\gamma_{R}$ preserves the top element because $\gamma_{R}(\top)=R[\top]=\{\top\}$
and the latter is the top element in $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$.
To see that it preserves binary meets we can compute
$$\gamma_{R}(x)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}\gamma_{R}(y)=R[x]\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}R[y]=\{z\wedge w\mid z\in R[x],w\in R[y]\}=R[x\wedge y]=\gamma_{R}(x\wedge y)$$Here the third equality follows from \ref{it:box-frm3} and \ref{it:box-frm4}
in Definition \ref{def:box-frm}.
Finally, $\gamma_{R}$ is continuous because the topology on $\mathfun{V}\mathbb{X}$ is
generated by subsets of the form ${\boxdot}a$, where $a\in A$, and their complements,
and $\gamma_{R}^{-1}({\boxdot}a)=m_{{\boxempty}}(a)\in A$ for all $a\in A$.
\end{proof}\par\clm Let $(\mathbb{X},\gamma)$ be a $(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$-dialgebra.
Let $(X,\leq,A)$ be the descriptive I-frame corresponding to $\mathbb{X}$ and
define the relation $R_{\gamma}$ on $X$ by $xR_{\gamma}y$ iff $y\in\gamma(x)$.
Then $(X,\leq,R,A)$ is a descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frame.
\begin{proof}This is indeed a general ${\boxempty}$-frame because for every clopen filter $a\in A$
we have $m_{{\boxempty}}(a)=\gamma^{-1}({\boxdot}a)\in\mathfun{Clp^{f}}\mathbb{X}=A$.
It is descriptive because $R_{\gamma}[x]=\gamma(x)$ is the intersection of
clopen filters.
\end{proof}\par It is easy to see that the two assignments define an isomorphism on objects:
We already know the correspondence for the underlying I-spaces (descriptive I-frames),
and on top of this we have $xRy$ iff $y\in\gamma_{R}(x)$ iff $xR_{\gamma_{R}}y$,
and $y\in\gamma(x)$ iff $xR_{\gamma}y$ iff $y\in\gamma_{R_{\gamma}}x$.
\par The verification of the isomorphism on morphisms is routine.
\end{proof}\par The proof of the following theorem was sketched at the start of this section.
\par\begin{thm}We have a dual equivalence of categories
$$\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}\equiv^{\op}\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}.$$\end{thm}\begin{proof}According to Theorem~\ref{thm:ISpace-Dialg} we have
$\mathsf{ISpace_{{\boxempty}}}\cong\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$.
It follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:V-I} that $\mathfun{V}$ is naturally isomorphic
to $\mathfun{I}$, so
$\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{V})$ is equivalent to $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{I})$.
The dual nature of dialgebras, together with the facts that $\mathfun{I}$ and
$\mathfun{j}$ are inclusion functors, implies that
$\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{I},\mathfun{I})$ is dual to $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{j},\mathfun{j})$.
Since $\mathfun{j}$ is naturally isomorphic to $\mathfun{N}$ the latter is
equivalent to $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$,
which in turn is isomorphic to $\mathsf{ISL_{{\boxempty}}}$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:ISLb-dialg}.
\end{proof}\par As a corollary we obtain completeness for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is complete with respect to ${\boxempty}$-models
and descriptive ${\boxempty}$-models.
\end{thm}\begin{proof}The proof of this theorem proceeds in a similar way as for $\mathbf{MI}$
in Section \ref{sec:base}.
\end{proof}\par The astute reader will have noticed that all functors used in the dialgebraic
perspective explained above are (naturally isomorphic to) inclusion functors.
We will encounter functors for which this is not the case in
Section~\ref{sec:monotone}.
\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conservative extensions}
\par There are many variations of modal intuitionistic logic,
and usually the ${\boxempty}$-modality satisfies the axioms \ref{ax:box1} and \ref{ax:box2}
(defined in Section \ref{sec:normal} above).
Therefore, the various semantics of these versions of modal intuitionistic
logic all provide sound semantics for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
In this section we show that in fact $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is complete with respect
to many of these semantics. This then implies that $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$
axiomatises the $(\top,\wedge,\to,{\boxempty})$-fragment of the modal intuitionistic
logics under consideration.
\par Our strategy for achieving this is simple: we show that ${\boxempty}$-frames
and -models are special cases of the semantics corresponding to various
variations of modal intuitionistic logic. Then, letting $\mathsf{K}$ denote
such a class of frames for modal intuitionistic logic, we argue that
$\mathsf{K}\Vdash\varphi$ implies $\mathsf{IF_{{\boxempty}}}\Vdash\varphi$,
which we know implies $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}\vdash\varphi$.
\par We make frequent use of the following proposition.
Here, if $R_{1}$ and $R_{2}$ are two relations on a set $X$, then we write
$R_{1}\circ R_{2}:=\{(x,z)\in X\times X\mid\exists y\in X\text{ s.t. }xR_{1}y\text{ and }yR_{2}z\}$.
\par\begin{prop}Let $(X,\leq,R)$ be a ${\boxempty}$-frame. Then we have
$$R=({\leq}\circ R\circ{\leq})\quad\text{ and }\quad({\leq}^{-1}\circ R)\subseteq(R\circ{\leq}^{-1}).$$\end{prop}\begin{proof}We first prove the left equality.
The inclusion from left to right follows from reflexivity of $\leq$. Furthermore,
it follows from \ref{it:box-frm2} that $R=(R\circ{\leq})$,
so it suffices to show that $({\leq}\circ R)\subseteq R$. In a diagram:
$$\hbox to73.54pt{\vbox to47.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.76923pt\lower-21.79718pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.83098pt}{18.68468pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{2.79985pt,1.59991pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -21.68 -13.8 L 20.65 13.8}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.97914pt}{-7.1361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$If $x\leq y$ then $x\wedge y=x$. Also, by \ref{it:box-frm1}
we have $xR\top$. It then follows from \ref{it:box-frm3} that
$(x\wedge y)R(z\wedge\top)$, i.e.~$xRz$.
\par The inclusion on the right follows from \ref{it:box-frm4}: if $xRz$ and $x\leq x^{\prime}$
then $(x\wedge x^{\prime})Rz$ so according to \ref{it:box-frm4} we can find a states $y,y^{\prime}$
such that $xRy$ and $x^{\prime}Ry^{\prime}$ and $y\wedge y^{\prime}=z$. The latter implies
$z\leq y^{\prime}$, so $y^{\prime}$ witnesses the desired inclusion.
\end{proof}\par We now consider various flavours of modal intuitionistic logic.
We write $\mathfun{Int}_{{\boxempty}}$ and
$\mathfun{Int}_{{\boxempty}\Diamond}$ for the extensions of
intuitionistic logic with ${\boxempty}$, and with ${\boxempty}$ and $\Diamond$,
respectively.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{The systems \texorpdfstring{$\mathbf{H\Box}$}{HBox} and \texorpdfstring{$\mathbf{H{\Box\Diamond}}$}{HBoxDiamond}
by Bo\v{z}i\'{c} and Do\v{s}en}
In \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BozDos84}{}{}]} the authors consider the logic $\mathbf{HK{\boxempty}}$,
which is an extension of intuitionistic logic with ${\boxempty}$ axiomatised by
\ref{ax:box1} and \ref{ax:box2}.
\par The interpreting structures are \emph{$H{\boxempty}$-frames} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BozDos84}{}{}, Definition 2]}
(found under a different name in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Pro12}{}{}, Definition 3.1]}).
A $H{\boxempty}$-frame is a tuple $(X,\leq,R)$ consisting of a pre-order $(X,\leq)$
and a relation $R$ on $X$ satisfying $({\leq}\circ R)\subseteq(R\circ{\leq})$.
Such a frame is called \emph{condensed} if $(R\circ{\leq})=R$ and
\emph{strictly condensed} if $({\leq}\circ R\circ{\leq})=R$.
They become \emph{$H{\boxempty}$-models} if one attaches to it a valuation
that interprets each proposition letter as an upset in $(X,\leq)$.
The box-modality is interpreted as usual, i.e.~a state $x$ satisfies
${\boxempty}\varphi$ if all its $R$-successors satisfy~$\varphi$.
\par It follows from Proposition \ref{prop:rels} and the fact that a valuation for
a ${\boxempty}$-frame sends a proposition letter to a filter, which in particular is
an upset, that every ${\boxempty}$-model is also a strictly condensed $H{\boxempty}$-model.
Therefore $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is sound and complete with respect to
the class of ((strictly) condensed) $H{\boxempty}$-frames and
$\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ characterises the $(\top,\wedge,\to,{\boxempty})$-fragment
of $\mathbf{HK{\boxempty}}$. As a consequence we have:
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{HK}{\boxempty}$ is a conservative extension of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\end{thm}\par Furthermore, in \lx@sectionsign 11 the authors also introduce the system $\mathbf{HK{{\boxempty}\Diamond}}$
for the language $\mathfun{Int}_{{\boxempty}\Diamond}$.
A semantics is given by the collection of so-called $H{{\boxempty}}{\Diamond}$-frames (models).
These are strictly condensed $H{\boxempty}$-frames (models) that additionally satisfy
$$({\geq}\circ R)\subseteq(R\circ{\geq}).$$Again, by Proposition \ref{prop:rels}, every ${\boxempty}$-model is a
$H{{\boxempty}\Diamond}$-model and $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ axiomatises the
$(\top,\wedge,\to,{\boxempty})$-fragment of $\mathbf{HK{{\boxempty}\Diamond}}$.
Therefore:
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{HK}{\boxempty}\Diamond$ is a conservative extension of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\end{thm}\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{The system \texorpdfstring{$\mathbf{IK}$}{IK} by Plotkin and Stirling}
In \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{PloSti86}{}{}]} the authors define an \emph{intuitionistic modal frame}
as a tuple $(X,\leq,R)$ consisting of a poset $(X,\leq)$ and a relation $R$
satisfying
$$({\geq}\circ R)\subseteq(R\circ{\geq})\quad\text{ and }\quad(R\circ{\leq})\subseteq({\leq}\circ R).$$These can be turned into models in the usual way.
While the diamond is interpreted as usual (i.e.~a state satisfies
$\Diamond\varphi$ if is has an $R$-successor satisfying $\varphi$),
persistence of ${\boxempty}$ is achieved by defining
$$x\Vdash{\boxempty}\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad x({\leq}\circ R)y\text{ implies }y\Vdash\varphi.$$When a frame satisfies $({\leq}\circ R)\subseteq R$ then reflexivity of
$\leq$ implies that $({\leq}\circ R)=R$.
In such cases, the interpretation of ${\boxempty}$ reduces to the usual one, i.e.
$$x\Vdash{\boxempty}\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad xRy\text{ implies }y\Vdash\varphi.$$This interpretation coincides with the one given by Fisher Servi in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Fis80}{}{}]}.
\par It follows from Proposition \ref{prop:rels} again that ${\boxempty}$-frames
are intuitionistic modal frames. Moreover, the fact that every ${\boxempty}$-frame
satisfies $R=({\leq}\circ R\circ{\leq})$ implies $({\leq}\circ R)\subseteq R$
so in case of ${\boxempty}$-frames our interpretation coincides with the one
used in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{PloSti86}{}{}]}.
Therefore we have completeness of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$
with respect to intuitionistic modal frames.
Again, soundness follows from the axiomatisation of the system
$\mathbf{IK}$ presented in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{PloSti86}{}{}]}.
So $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ axiomatises the
$(\top,\wedge,\to,{\boxempty})$-fragment of $\mathbf{IK}$.
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{IK}$ is a conservative extension of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\end{thm}\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{The system \texorpdfstring{$\mathbf{IntK_{\Box}}$}{IntKBox} by Wolter and Zakharyaschev}
The frames used in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{WolZak98}{}{}, Section 2]} to interpret
$\mathfun{Int}_{{\boxempty}}$
are posets $(X,\leq)$ with an additional relation $R$ satisfying
$R=({\leq}\circ R\circ{\leq})$.
(The same frames have also been used in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Yok85}{}{}]} and~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Koj11}{}{}]}.)
Formulae are interpreted as upsets
in $(X,\leq)$ and models are obtained by adding to a frame a valuation
that sends each proposition letter to an up-closed subset of the frame.
The axiomatisation of $\mathbf{IntK_{{\boxempty}}}$ proves
the axioms ${\boxempty}\varphi\wedge{\boxempty}\psi={\boxempty}(\varphi\wedge\psi)$ and ${\boxempty}\top=\top$.
It can be proved similarly as above that $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is sound
and complete with respect to their models, and that
$\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ axiomatises the $(\top,\wedge,\to,{\boxempty})$-fragment
of $\mathbf{IntK_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{IntK_{{\boxempty}}}$ is a conservative extension of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
\end{thm}\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Dialgebraic perspective}
\par We have seen in Sections \ref{sec:normal} and \ref{sec:duality} that the
categories of ${\boxempty}$-frames, descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frames and ISLOs are all
categories of dialgebras.
In this section we will see that in fact $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is an instance
of a \emph{dialgebraic logic} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]}.
Using general results from {\it op.~\!cit.}~we
then re-obtain the completeness result for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
Furthermore, the dialgebraic view allows us to transfer the general
expressivity-somewhere-else result for dialgebraic logics from
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Section 7]} to the setting of modal meet-implication logic:
Two states $x$ and $x^{\prime}$ in a ${\boxempty}$-frame are logically equivalent if and only if
corresponding states $\eta(x)$ and $\eta(x^{\prime})$ in the so-called
\emph{filter extension} are behaviourally equivalent.
\par In Section \ref{sec:monotone} we use the dialgebraic perspective
to succinctly present the extension of meet-implication logic with
a monotone unary modality.
\par We begin by recalling the framework of dialgebraic logic, specialised
to our setup.
In an effort to avoid notational clutter, we work in a
setting without proposition letters.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Dialgebraic logic}
We sketch the basic setup of dialgebraic logic \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Section 5]}
specialised to our base logic. This starts with
a dual adjunction and a restriction thereof. In our case:
$$\hbox to90.93pt{\vbox to57.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.46373pt\lower-30.09299pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-45.46373pt}{-19.80412pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.1712pt}{-26.94022pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$Recall that $\mathsf{MF}$ is simply the same category of $\mathsf{SL}$,
but viewed as frame semantics.
Both units of the dual adjunction in the top row are given by
$\eta_{X}:X\to\mathfun{F}X:x\mapsto\widetilde{x}=\{a\in\mathfun{F}X\mid x\in a\}$.
The functors $\mathfun{F}_{1}$ and $\mathfun{F}_{2}$ are restrictions of $\mathfun{F}$
to $\mathsf{IF}$ and $\mathsf{ISL}$ respectively.
It follows from Corollary \ref{cor:imp-imp} that $\mathfun{F}_{1}$ and $\mathfun{F}_{2}$
are well defined on objects.
An easy computation shows that applying $\mathfun{F}$ to an I-frame morphism
yields an $\mathsf{ISL}$-homomorphism, so $\mathfun{F}_{1}$ is well defined.
For the converse, we note that $\mathfun{F}_{2}=\mathfun{U}_{\mathsf{IF}}\circ\mathfun{F^{t}}$,
where $\mathfun{U}_{\mathsf{IF}}:\mathsf{ISpace}\to\mathsf{IF}$ is the obvious forgetful
functor. This implies that $\mathfun{F}_{2}$ is well defined.
\par Furthermore, the latter proves that for every implicative semilattice $A$,
the unit $\eta_{A}:A\to\mathfun{F}_{1}\mathfun{F}_{2}A$ is an implicative semilattice
homomorphism.
In the terminology of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]} this means that the setup is
\emph{well structured}, and this is a prerequisite for many of the results
in {\it op.~\!cit}.
\par Let $\mathfun{T}:\mathsf{IF}\to\mathsf{MF}$ be a functor.
A \emph{modal logic} for $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$ is a pair
$(\mathfun{L},\rho)$ where $\mathfun{L}$ is a functor $\mathsf{ISL}\to\mathsf{SL}$
and $\rho$ is a natural transformation $\rho:\mathfun{LF}_{1}\to\mathfun{FT}$.
In a diagram:

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$We assume the existence of an initial object $(\Psi,\ell)$ in $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$,
which plays the r\^{o}le of Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra.
(We construct an explicit example of the initial dialgebra in case $\mathfun{L}=\mathfun{N}$ below.)
The interpretation
of formulae in an $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra $(X,\gamma)$ is then given
by the unique morphism $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\gamma}:\Psi\to X$ in $\mathsf{ISL}$
making the following diagram commute:
