$$\rho_{(X,\leq)}({\boxempty}a)\mapsto\{p\in\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)\mid p\subseteq a\}.$$The initial dialgebra in $\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$ is the
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra corresponding to $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$.
Explicitly, write $\Psi$ for this Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra
(i.e.~${L}_{{\boxempty}}$-formulae modulo provable equivalence) and let
$[\varphi]$ denote the equivalence class of a formula $\varphi$.
Then we can define an $(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$-dialgebra structure on $\Psi$
by setting $\ell({\boxempty}[\varphi])=[{\boxempty}\varphi]$. For any $(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$-dialgebra
$(A,\alpha)$ the map that sends $[\top]\in\Psi$ to $\top_{A}$, the top element in $A$,
uniquely extends to an $(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$-dialgebra morphism
$i:(\Psi,\ell)\to(A,\alpha)$ via
\@@amsalign
In case $(A,\alpha)$ is the complex dialgebra of a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra
$(X,\gamma)$, the meet in $A$ is given by intersection, and implication by
$$i([\varphi])\xrightarrow{\circ}_{A}i([\psi])=\{x\in X\mid\text{if }x\leq y\text{ and }y\in i([\varphi])\text{ then }y\in i([\psi])\}.$$\par By construction, for any $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra $(X,\leq,\gamma)$
the following commutes:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$Let us compute explicitly what this means for the interpretation of
a formula of the form ${\boxempty}\varphi$.
The interpretation $\llbracket{\boxempty}\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}$ of ${\boxempty}\varphi$ in $(X,\gamma)$
is then given by $\mathfun{j}\llbracket\ell({\boxempty}\varphi)\rrbracket_{\gamma}$.
By commutativity of the diagram this equals
\@@amsalign
Indeed, a state $x$ satisfies ${\boxempty}\varphi$ iff all of its successors (all $y\in\gamma(x)$)
satisfy $\varphi$, as desired.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Logic via predicate liftings and axioms}
A convenient way to think about dialgebraic logics is via predicate liftings
and axioms.
Given a collection of so-called predicate liftings and axioms
between them, we can define a logic $(\mathfun{L},\rho)$.
\par A (unary) \emph{predicate lifting} is a natural transformation
$$\lambda:\mathfun{UFi}\to\mathfun{UFT},$$where $\mathfun{U}:\mathsf{SL}\to\mathsf{Set}$ denotes the forgetful functor.
For a collection $\Lambda$ of predicate liftings for $\mathfun{T}$ and a set $X$,
let $\mathfun{L}^{\prime}X$ be the free semilattice generated by the set
$$\big\{\heartsuit^{\lambda}v\mid\lambda\in\Lambda,v\in\Var\big\}.$$By a \emph{$\Lambda$-axiom} we mean a pair
$(\varphi,\psi)$ of two elements in $\mathfun{L}^{\prime}\Var$, where $\Var$ is some set of variables.
\par Let $\Lambda$ be a set of predicate liftings and $\Ax$ a collection of
$\Lambda$-axioms.
The functor $\mathfun{L}$ then arises by sending an implicative meet-semilattice $A$
to the free semilattice generated by $\{\heartsuit^{\lambda}(a)\mid a\in A\}$
modulo the relations $R$ from $\Ax$, where the variables are substituted for elements of $A$.
For an $\mathsf{ISL}$-homomorphism $h:A\to B$, the morphism
$\mathfun{L}f$ is defined on generators by
$\mathfun{L}f(\heartsuit^{\lambda}a)=\heartsuit^{\lambda}f(a)$.
The induced natural transformation $\rho$ is given on components by
$\rho_{(X,\leq)}(\heartsuit^{\lambda}a)=\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(a)$.
\par A collection $\Ax$ of axioms is said to be \emph{sound} if it induces
a well-defined natural transformation
$\rho_{(\Lambda,\Ax)}:\mathfun{LP^{\prime}}\to\mathfun{PT}$ via
$$\rho_{(\Lambda,\Ax),X}([\heartsuit^{\lambda}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})]_{R})=\lambda_{X}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})$$that is independent of the choice of representative relative to
$R$-equivalence classes $[\cdot]_{R}$.
If this is well defined, naturality is
an immediate consequence of naturality of the predicate liftings.
\par In the case of $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$,
we can view the modality ${\boxempty}$ as being induced by the predicate lifting
$\lambda:\mathfun{UFi}\to\mathfun{UFG}$ given by
$$\lambda_{(X,\leq)}:\mathfun{UFi}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{UFG}(X,\leq):p\mapsto\{q\in\mathfun{G}A\mid q\subseteq p\}.$$Let us temporarily write $\heartsuit^{\lambda}$ for the modal operator
induced by $\lambda$.
For a ${\boxempty}$-frame $\mathcal{M}=(X,\leq,\gamma)$ we then have
$$\mathcal{M},x\Vdash\heartsuit^{\lambda}\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\gamma(x)\in\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket)\quad\text{iff}\quad\gamma(x)\subseteq\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket.$$Recognising $\gamma(x)$ as the successor-set of $x$, this shows that
$x$ satisfies $\heartsuit^{\lambda}\varphi$ if all its successors satisfy $\varphi$.
So $\heartsuit^{\lambda}$ coincides with ${\boxempty}$ as defined above.
\par We verify that $\lambda$ is indeed a well-defined natural transformation.
\par\begin{prop}$\lambda:\mathfun{UFi}\to\mathfun{UFG}$ is a well-defined natural transformation.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}Let $(X,\leq)$ be an I-frame and $p\in\mathfun{UFi}(X,\leq)$.
Since $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$ is ordered by reverse inclusion
the set $\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p)$ is up-closed in $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$.
Moreover, if $q,r\in\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p)$, then $q\cup r\subseteq p$.
This implies $q\wedge_{\mathfun{G}}r\subseteq p$,
so $\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p)$ is closed under meets in $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$,
and hence $\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p)$ is a filter in $\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$.
Next we show that $\lambda_{(X,\leq)}$ is a homomorphism in $\mathsf{SL}$.
Clearly $\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(X)=\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$, so top is preserved.
Let $p,q$ be filters in $(X,\leq)$, then
$$\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p\cap q)=\{r\mid r\subseteq p\cap q\}=\{r\mid r\subseteq p\}\cap\{r\mid r\subseteq q\}=\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(p)\cap\lambda_{(X,\leq)}(q).$$Finally, for naturality, let $h:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ be an
I-frame morphism and let $p^{\prime}$ be a filter in $(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ and
$q\in\mathfun{G}(X,\leq)$. Then we have
\@@amsalign
so indeed $\lambda$ is a natural transformation.
\end{proof}\par Together with the axioms \ref{ax:box1} and \ref{ax:box2}
we recover the functor $\mathfun{N}:\mathsf{ISL}\to\mathsf{SL}$.
\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Instatiating the theory of Dialgebraic Logic}
Now that we have set up the logic $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ in a dialgebraic setting,
we can exploit this by using theorems from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]}
to obtain results for $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ interpreted in ${\boxempty}$-frames.
\par We start by re-obtaining completeness.
By Propositions 6.9 and 6.10 in {\it op.~\!cit.}~it suffices
to prove that the adjoint buddy $\rho^{\flat}:\mathfun{GF}_{2}\to\mathfun{FN}$

We shall establish a stronger statement: we can find
a \emph{natural} transformation $\tau:\mathfun{FN}\to\mathfun{GF}_{2}$ such that
$\rho^{\flat}\circ\tau=\id$.
Guided by the duality for for $(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$-dialgebras from
Section~\ref{sec:duality}, for $A\in\mathsf{ISL}$ define
$$\tau_{A}:\mathfun{FN}A\to\mathfun{GF}_{2}A:U\mapsto\{p\in\mathfun{F}_{2}A\mid\text{if }{\boxempty}a\in U\text{ then }a\in p\}.$$This is easily seen to send $U$ to
the upwards closure of the filter $p_{U}=\{a\in A\mid{{\boxempty}}a\in U\}\in\mathfun{F}_{2}A$.
Therefore $\tau_{A}$ is well defined, i.e.~it lands in $\mathfun{GF}_{2}A$.
It clearly preserves the top element, and it preserves binary meets because
$$\tau_{A}(U)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}\tau_{A}(V)={\uparrow}_{\subseteq}p_{U}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}{\uparrow}_{\subseteq}p_{V}={\uparrow}_{\subseteq}(p_{U}\cap p_{V})={\uparrow}_{\subseteq}(p_{U\cap V})=\tau_{A}(U\cap V).$$\par\begin{prop}The assignment $\tau=(\tau_{A})_{A\in\mathsf{ISL}}:\mathfun{FN}\to\mathfun{GF}_{2}$
defines a natural transformation and satisfies $\rho^{\flat}\circ\tau=\id_{\mathfun{FN}}$.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}We first prove naturality. This means that we have to show that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-56.21114pt}{-1.68054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.6849pt}{-1.93054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$commutes for any implicative meet-semilattice homomorphism $h:A\to B$.
Suppose $U\in\mathfun{FN}B$ and $p\in\tau_{A}(\mathfun{FN}h(U))$.
In order to show that $p\in\mathfun{GF}_{2}h(\tau_{B}(U))$ we need to find
$q\in\tau_{B}(U)$ such that $\mathfun{F}_{2}h(q)=p$. Since $\mathfun{F}_{2}h$ is
a bounded morphism, it suffices to prove that there exists
$q\in\tau_{B}(U)$ such that $\mathfun{F}_{2}h(q)\subseteq p$.
Consider $q_{U}=\{b\in B\mid{\boxempty}b\in U\}$.
By construction $q_{U}\in\tau_{B}(U)$.
Furthermore, $a\in\mathfun{F}_{2}h(q_{U})$ implies $h(a)\in q_{U}$,
hence ${\boxempty}h(a)\in U$ so $a\in\mathfun{FN}h(U)$ and since
$p\in\tau_{A}(\mathfun{FN}h(U))$ this entails that $a\in p$.
So $\mathfun{F}_{2}h(q_{U})\subseteq p$, as desired.
\par Conversely, suppose $p\in\mathfun{GF}_{2}h(\tau_{B}(U))$.
Then $p=\mathfun{F}_{2}h(q)$ for some $q\in\tau_{B}(U)$.
Also, $a\in\mathfun{FN}h(U)$ implies $h(a)\in U$, hence $h(a)\in q$ which
in turn implies $a\in p$. Therefore $p\in\tau_{A}(\mathfun{FN}h(U))$, as desired.
This proves commutativity of the diagram, and hence naturality of $\tau$.
\par In order to prove that $\rho^{\flat}_{A}\circ\tau_{A}=\id$ we first note
that for any $U\in\mathfun{F}NA$ and $a\in A$ we have
$\tau(U)\subseteq\widetilde{a}$ if and only if ${\boxempty}a\in U$.
The direction from right to left follows from the definition of $\tau$.
For the converse direction suppose ${\boxempty}a\notin U$,
then $p_{U}$ is such that $p_{U}\in\tau(U)$ while $p_{U}\notin\widetilde{a}$,
so that $\tau(U)\not\subseteq\widetilde{a}$.
\par Now to see that $\rho^{\flat}_{A}\circ\tau_{A}=\id$, note that elements of
$\mathfun{FN}A$ are filters of $\mathfun{N}A$, and these are uniquely determined
by the collection of elements of the form ${\boxempty}a$ they contain, where $a\in A$.
We now have
$${\boxempty}a\in\rho^{\flat}(\tau(U))\quad\text{iff}\quad\tau(U)\subseteq\widetilde{a}\quad\text{iff}\quad{\boxempty}a\in U.$$The first ``iff'' follows from unravelling the definition of $\rho^{\flat}$.
\end{proof}\par In \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Definition 6.1]}, a logic $(\mathfun{L},\rho)$ for $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebras is called
\emph{complete} if the source
\begin{equation}\big\{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\gamma}:\Psi\to\mathfun{F}_{1}(X,\leq)\big\}_{(X,\leq,\gamma)\in\mathsf{Dialg}(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})}\end{equation}is jointly monic. Intuitively, this means that for every two non-equivalent
formulae $\varphi,\psi\in\Psi$ we can find a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra
$(X,\leq,\gamma)$ in which $\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket_{\gamma}\neq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{\gamma}$.
In our setup this reduces to the usual notion of (weak) completeness.
As a corollary of Propositions 6.9 and 6.10 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]}
we get:
\par\begin{thm}The system $\mathbf{MI_{{\boxempty}}}$ is complete with respect to
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebras.
\end{thm}\par Concretely, $\tau$ allows us to transform a $(\mathfun{N},\mathfun{j})$-dialgebra $(A,\alpha)$
into a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra whose complex algebra has $(A,\alpha)$
as a subalgebra. Unravelling the constructions
shows that this is precisely the $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra
underlying the dual of descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frame of $(A,\alpha)$.
\par Letting $(X,\leq,\gamma,\Psi)$ be the dual dual of $(\Psi,\ell)$
then gives rise to a $\mathfun{G}$-coalgebra $(X,\leq,\gamma)$
such that $(\Psi,\ell)$ is a subalgebra of the complex algebra
$(\mathfun{F}_{1}(X,\leq),\gamma^{*})$ of $(X,\leq,\gamma)$.
In particular this implies the existence of a monomorphism $m:\Psi\to\mathfun{F}_{1}X$
such that

Initiality of $(\Psi,\ell)$ then implies that $m=\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\gamma}:\Psi\to\mathfun{F}_{1}(X,\leq)$, and its monotonicity witnesses that (joint) monotonicity
of the source in \eqref{eq:source}.
\par Apart from completeness, the natural transformation $\tau$ entails results for
expressivity and filter extensions. We call a ${\boxempty}$-frame \emph{expressive}
if two states are logically equivalent if and only if they are behaviourally
equivalent.
If we translate this to the abstract dialgebraic setting, we get that an
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is
\emph{expressive} if the theory map $\operatorname{th}_{\gamma}$ given by

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$factors through a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra morphism followed
by a monomorphism in $\mathsf{IF}$.
Intuitively, the theory map sends a state in a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra
to the collection of formulae it satisfies.
Monotonicity of the second part of the factorisation implies that
logically equivalent states must already be identified by the dialgebra
morphism.
\par Such a strong property is not always attainable.
A weaker condition is expressivity-somewhere-else:
A ${\boxempty}$-frame $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is said to be expressive-somewhere-else if there
exists an expressive ${\boxempty}$-frame $(Y,\leq_{Y},\delta)$ and a monotone
truth-preserving map $f:X\to Y$.
In that case, two states $x,x^{\prime}\in X$ are logically equivalent if and only if
$f(x)$ and $f(x^{\prime})$ are behaviourally equivalent (in $(Y,\leq_{Y},\delta)$).
In general, a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is said to be
\emph{expressive-somewhere-else} if the theory map $\operatorname{th}_{\gamma}$
factors through a morphism in $\mathsf{MF}$ followed by the theory
map of an expressive $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{T})$-dialgebra.
\par As a consequence of Proposition \ref{prop:i-G-tau} and
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Theorem 7.5]} we have:
\par\begin{thm}Every descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frame is expressive, and every
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra is expressive-somewhere-else.
\end{thm}\par In classical modal logic, the r\^{o}le of $(Y,\leq_{Y},\delta)$ is played
by \emph{ultrafilter extensions},
and in the context of intuitionistic logic by \emph{prime filter extensions}
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Gol05}{}{}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Section 7]}.
In the setting of the current paper, the r\^{o}le of $(Y,\leq_{Y},\delta)$ is
fulfilled by the \emph{filter extension} of $(X,\leq,\gamma)$.
Chasing the definitions from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]},
we find that the filter extension of a $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra
$(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is the $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra underlying the
descriptive ${\boxempty}$-frame dual to the complex algebra $(\mathfun{F}_{1}(X,\leq),\gamma^{*})$.
Concretely, the filter extension of $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ has
state-space $(\widehat{X},\subseteq)=\mathfun{F}_{2}\mathfun{F}_{1}(X,\leq)$ and its
structure map given by
$$\widehat{\gamma}:\mathfun{i}(\widehat{X},\subseteq)\to\mathfun{G}(\widehat{X},\subseteq):p\mapsto\bigcap\{\widetilde{a}\subseteq\widehat{X}\mid\gamma^{-1}({\boxdot}a)\in p\}.$$Here ${\boxdot}a=\{b\in\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)\mid b\subseteq a\}$,
so that $\gamma^{-1}({\boxdot}a)\in\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)$.
\par As a consequence of the results in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Section 7]} the
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{G})$-dialgebra $(\widehat{X},\subseteq,\widehat{\gamma})$ is
expressive, and the theory map $\operatorname{th}_{\gamma}$ of $(X,\gamma)$ of factors via

Thus two states $x,x^{\prime}\in X$ are logically equivalent if and only if
$\eta_{X}(x)$ and $\eta_{X}(x^{\prime})$ are behaviourally equivalent when
conceived of as states in $(\widehat{X},\subseteq,\widehat{\gamma})$.
\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{A monotone modality}
\par In this section we discuss the enrichment of $\mathbf{MI}$ with a
monotone modal operator, that we denote by ${\vartriangle}$.
We present this dialgebraically, and leave explicit descriptions of
the logical axioms, frame semantics (a variation of neighbourhood semantics)
and descriptive frames to the reader.
\par The semantics of this logic are given by a simple adaptation of
monotone neighbourhood frames, see e.g.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Che80,Han03}{}{}]}.
\par\defi Let $(X,\leq)$ be an I-frame. Define
$\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)=\{W\subseteq\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)\mid\text{ if }a\in W\text{ and }a\subseteq b\text{ then }b\in W\}$.
If we order $\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)$ by inclusion then it forms a semilattice
with intersection as meet and the whole of $\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)$ as top element.
For an I-frame morphism $h:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$,
define $\mathfun{H}h:\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{H}(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ by
$\mathfun{H}h(W)=\{a^{\prime}\in\mathfun{F}(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})\mid h^{-1}(a^{\prime})\in W\}$.
\par Intuitively, $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebras are I-frames with an additional
map that assigns to each world $x$ an
up-closed collection of neighbourhoods, and the neighbourhoods are
filters of $(X,\leq)$.
We now define our intended logic as a dialgebraic logic for
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebras.
\par\defi The predicate lifting $\lambda^{{\vartriangle}}:\mathfun{UFi}\to\mathfun{UFH}$
is given on components by
$$\lambda^{{\vartriangle}}_{(X,\leq)}(a)=\{W\in\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)\mid a\in W\}.$$We abbreviate ${\vartriangle}a=\heartsuit^{\lambda^{{\vartriangle}}}a$, and work
with a single axiom: ${\vartriangle}(a\wedge b)\leq{\vartriangle}a$.
\par It is obvious that $\lambda^{{\vartriangle}}_{(X,\leq)}(a)$ is a filter for all I-frames
$(X,\leq)$ and filters $a$. (It is nonempty because the collection of all
filters is in it, it is up-closed because $a\in W\subseteq W^{\prime}$ implies
$a\in W^{\prime}$, and it is closed under meets because meets are given by
intersection and $a\in W$ and $a\in W^{\prime}$ implies $a\in W\cap W^{\prime}$.)
\par\begin{prop}The assignment $\lambda^{{\vartriangle}}$ is a natural transformation.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}Let $f:(X,\leq)\to(X^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})$ be an I-frame morphism.
We need to show that

\@@amsalign
This proves that the diagram commutes.
\end{proof}\par The dialgebraic logic given by this predicate lifting and axiom
is the pair $(\mathfun{M},\rho)$.
The functor $\mathfun{M}:\mathsf{ISL}\to\mathsf{SL}$ is given on objects
by sending an implicative meet-semilattice $A$ to the free meet-semilattice
generated by $\{{\vartriangle}a\mid a\in A\}$ modulo ${\vartriangle}(a\wedge b)\leq{\vartriangle}a$.
The action of $\mathfun{M}$ on a morphism $h:A\to B$ is defined on generators
by $\mathfun{M}h({\vartriangle}a)={\vartriangle}h(a)$.
The natural transformation $\rho$ is given by
$$\rho_{(X,\leq)}:\mathfun{MF}_{1}(X,\leq)\to\mathfun{FH}(X,\leq):{\vartriangle}a\mapsto\{W\in\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)\mid a\in W\}.$$\par\begin{prop}The assignment $\rho$ is well defined, hence a natural transformation.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}Let $(X,\leq)$ be an I-frame and $a,b$ filters in it.
We have $a\wedge b\leq a$ so for all $W\in\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)$,
$a\wedge b\in W$ implies $a\in W$.
Therefore
$$\rho_{(X,\leq)}({\vartriangle}(a\wedge b))=\{W\in\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)\mid a\wedge b\in W\}\subseteq\{W\in\mathfun{H}(X,\leq)\mid a\in W\}=\rho_{(X,\leq)}({\vartriangle}a).$$So $\rho$ is well defined and the dialgebraic logic is sound.
\end{proof}\par As we have seen in Section \ref{sec:dialg}, in order to obtain completeness,
filter extensions and expressivity-somewhere-else, it suffices to find a
natural transformation $\tau:\mathfun{FM}\to\mathfun{HF}_{2}$
such that $\rho^{\flat}\circ\tau=\id$.
We complete this section by giving such a $\tau$.

of $\rho^{\flat}$. This is given on components by the concatenation

\@@amsalign
\par In our definition of $\tau$ we require the notions of a clopen
and closed filter. We call a filter on $\mathfun{F}_{2}A$ (i.e.~an element
of $\mathfun{FF}_{2}A$) \emph{clopen} if it is of the form
$\widetilde{a}=\{x\in\mathfun{F}_{2}A\mid a\in x\}$
and \emph{closed} if it is the intersection of all clopen filters in
which it is contained (cf.~Definition \ref{def:fun-V}).
\par\defi Let $A$ be an implicative semilattice and $U\in\mathfun{FM}A$. Then we define
$\tau_{A}(U)\in\mathfun{HF}_{2}A$ by:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For all clopen filters $\widetilde{a}\in\mathfun{FF}_{2}A$, let
$\widetilde{a}\in\tau_{A}(U)$ if ${\vartriangle}a\in U$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For all closed filters $c$, let $c\in\tau_{A}(U)$ if all clopen filters
containing $c$ are in $\tau_{A}(U)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $p$ is any filter, let $p\in\tau_{A}(U)$ if there is a closed filter $c$
such that $c\subseteq p$ and $c\in\tau_{A}(U)$.
\end{itemize}This yields an assignment $\tau:\mathfun{FM}\to\mathfun{HF}_{2}$.
\par It is trivial to see that $\tau_{A}$ is well defined for every implicative semilattice $A$.
\par\begin{lem}For each $A\in\mathsf{ISL}$, the map $\tau_{A}$ is an M-frame morphism.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}First we prove that $\tau_{A}$ preserves top elements.
The top element of $\mathfun{FM}A$ is $\mathfun{M}A$ and
we observe that $\{\top\}\in\tau_{A}(\mathfun{M}A)$ because
$\{\top\}=\bigcap\{\widetilde{a}\mid a\in A\}$.
Furthermore this implies $\{\top\}$ is closed. Since every other filter
of $A$ is a superset of $\{\top\}$ this implies that
$\tau_{A}(\mathfun{M}A)=\mathfun{F}_{2}A$, which is the top element of
$\mathfun{HF}_{2}A$.
\par We now prove that $\tau_{A}$ preserves meets.
Let $U,V\in\mathfun{FM}A$. We have to prove that
$$\tau_{A}(U)\cap\tau_{A}(V)=\tau_{A}(U\cap V).$$If $p\in\tau_{A}(U\cap V)$ then
there is a closed filter $c\subseteq p$ such that for all clopen filters
$\widetilde{a}$ containing $c$, ${\vartriangle}a\in U\cap V$. Clearly this implies
that $c\in\tau_{A}(U)$ and $c\in\tau_{A}(V)$, hence $p\in\tau_{A}(U)\cap\tau_{A}(V)$.
Conversely, suppose $p\in\tau_{A}(U)\cap\tau_{A}(V)$. Then there are closed
filters $c_{U}\in\tau_{A}(U)$ and $c_{V}\in\tau_{A}(V)$ that are both contained
in $p$.
Let $c=\bigcap\{\widetilde{a}\mid c_{U}\subseteq\widetilde{a}\text{ and }c_{V}\subseteq\widetilde{a}\}$.
Then we know from the duality for the Vietoris filter functor that
$c=c_{U}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\hbox{$\wedge$}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip-3.33334pt\raisebox{4.05557pt}{\hbox{$\wedge$}}\hskip-3.33334pt\hskip 3.33334pt}}c_{V}=\{x\cap y\mid x\in c_{U},y\in c_{V}\}$.
But since both $c_{U}$ and $c_{V}$ are subsets of $p$ and $p$ is a filter
(hence closed under $\cap$, which acts as meet in $\mathfun{F}_{2}A$)
this implies that $c\subseteq p$.
By construction $c\in\tau_{A}(U\cap V)$,
so as a consequence we have $p\in\tau_{A}(U\cap V)$.
\end{proof}\par\par\begin{lem}$\tau$ is natural.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}Let $f:A\to B$ be an implicative semilattice morphism. We need to show that

Let $U\in\mathfun{FM}B$ and $p\in\mathfun{FF}_{2}A$.
Since elements of $\mathfun{HF}_{2}A$ are uniquely determined by the clopen filters
they contain, it suffices to prove that
$\widetilde{a}\in\tau_{A}((\mathfun{M}f)^{-1}(U))$ if and only if
$\widetilde{a}\in\mathfun{H}f^{-1}(\tau_{B}(U))$ for all $a\in A$.
To see that this is indeed the case, compute

So $\tau$ is a natural transformation.
\end{proof}\par We now show that $\tau$ is right inverse to $\rho^{\flat}$.
\par\begin{prop}For all $A\in\mathsf{ISL}$, the composition
$$\hbox to187.52pt{\vbox to20.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 93.75938pt\lower-7.82639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{

\end{prop}\begin{proof}We have seen that ${\vartriangle}a\in\rho^{\flat}(W)$ iff $\widetilde{a}\in W$.
Since a filter in $\mathfun{FM}A$ is determined uniquely by the elements
of the form ${\vartriangle}a$ it contains (where $a\in A$), it suffices to
show that for all $U\in\mathfun{FM}A$ we have
\begin{equation}{\vartriangle}a\in U\quad\text{iff}\quad\widetilde{a}\in\tau_{A}(U).\end{equation}The direction from left to right holds by definition.
Conversely, suppose $\widetilde{a}\in\tau_{A}(U)$.
Then there exists a closed filter $c\in\tau_{A}(U)$ such that $c\subseteq\widetilde{a}$.
But this implies ${\vartriangle}a\in U$ by definition of $\tau$.
\end{proof}\par As a consequence of the existence of such a $\tau$, we get
completeness, filter extensions and expressivity-somewhere-else.
Concretely, the filter extension of an $(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebra
$(X,\leq,\gamma)$ is based on the implicative semilattice
$(\widehat{X},\subseteq)=\mathfun{FF}(X,\leq)$.
The dialgebra structure $\widehat{\gamma}$ on $(\widehat{X},\subseteq)$ is given as follows.
For a filter $p\in\widehat{X}$ and a filter
$U\in\mathfun{F}(\widehat{X},\subseteq)$ we define:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $U$ is a clopen filter, i.e.~if $U=\widetilde{a}$ for some
$a\in\mathfun{F}(X,\leq)$,
then $U\in\widehat{\gamma}(p)$ iff $\{x\in X\mid a\in\gamma(x)\}\in p$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $U$ is a closed filter, then $U\in\widehat{\gamma}(p)$ iff all
clopen filters containing $U$ are in $\widehat{\gamma}(p)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@If $U$ is any filter, then $U\in\widehat{\gamma}(p)$ iff there
is a closed filter $C$ such that $C\subseteq U$ and $C\in\widehat{\gamma}(p)$.
\end{itemize}Expressivity-somewhere else then states:
\par\begin{thm}Two states $x$ and $y$
in $(X,\leq,\gamma)$ are logically equivalent if and only if
$\eta_{(X,\leq)}(x),\eta_{(X,\leq)}(y)\in(\widehat{X},\subseteq)$ are
behaviourally equivalent. That is, if they are identified by some
$(\mathfun{i},\mathfun{H})$-dialgebra morphism.
\end{thm}\par As explained in Section \ref{sec:dialg}, this theorem is a consequence
of general results in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}]}.
\par\begin{rem}It can be proved in a similar manner as in Section \ref{sec:fragments} that the system
$\mathbf{MI_{{\vartriangle}}}=\mathbf{MI}+{\vartriangle}(a\wedge b)\leq{\vartriangle}a$
characterises the meet-implication-modality-fragment of
intuitionistic logic with a \emph{geometric modality} from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Gol93}{}{}, Section 6]}.
The latter was called monotone modal intuitionistic logic in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GroPat20}{}{}, Section 8]}.
\end{rem}\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par We have started investigating modal extensions of the $(\top,\wedge,\to)$-fragment
of intuitionistic logic, and shown that this fits in the framework
of dialgebraic logic.
We list some potential directions for further research.
\par\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Different modal operators}
While we have given two modal extensions of $\mathbf{MI}$, there
may be many other interesting ones. For example, it would be interesting
to investigate modalities of higher arities,
or a weak negation modality like in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Cel99}{}{}]}.
\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Kripke-style bisimulation}
Another equivalence notion between ${\boxempty}$-frames, besides behavioural equivalence,
is given by Kripke-style bisimulation. While it is clear that
bisimilar states are logically equivalent, it would be interesting
to investigate the converse (and thus derive a Hennessy-Milner result).
Similar investigations can be carried out for the extension
of $\mathbf{MI}$ with a monotone operator,
where the notion of bisimulation should be inspired by the one for
monotone modal logic over a classical base \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Han03,HanKup04}{}{}]}.
\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{Co- and contravariant functors}
In many examples of modal logic based on a dual adjunction,
the contravariant functor turning frames into algebras has a
\emph{covariant} counterpart:
For classical modal logic we have a co- and contravariant powerset functor;
In positive modal logic, taking the collection of up-closed subsets of a poset
gives rise to both a co- and a contravariant functor.
The same happens here, where the contravariant filter functor $\mathfun{F}$
and the covariant filter functor $\mathfun{G}$ (that can be extended to $\mathsf{MF}$)
coincide on objects.
This raises the question whether these are instances of a more
general phenomenon. Perhaps one can identify those dual adjunction
that allow for such a covariant counterpart.

\par\endthebibliography{}\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{A Hilbert-style system for meet-implication logic}
\par We present a Hilbert-style axiom system for the meet-implication
fragment of intuitionistic propositional logic and establish
soundness and completeness with respect to an equational
axiomatisation. We consider the $\lor$-free fragment of
propositional logic over a set $V$ of propositional variables.
More precisely, the language $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}$ is given by the grammar
$$\varphi::=\top\mid p\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\to\varphi$$where $p\in\Prop$ ranges over the set of propositional variables. We
use standard operator precedence and assume that $\land$ binds
more tightly than $\to$.
\par\par\defi The Hilbert-style deductive system for the meet-implication
fragment of intuitionistic propositional logic is given by the
axioms
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$a\to(b\to a)$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$(a\to(b\to c))\to((a\to b)\to(a\to c))$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$(a\wedge b)\to a$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$(a\wedge b)\to b$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$a\to(b\to(a\wedge b))$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\top$
\end{enumerate}If $\Gamma$ is a set of formulae and $a$ is a formula, we
say that $a$ \emph{is deducible in ${H}$} from $\Gamma$
if $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a$ can be derived using the
rules
$$\mbox{(\assum)}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}a}(\mbox{if } a \in\Gamma)
\hskip 20.44434pt\hskip 10.22217pt\mbox{($\mathrm{ax}$)}\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}a} (\mbox{if } a \in H)
\hskip 20.44434pt\hskip 10.22217pt\mbox{({mp})}\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{H}a \hskip 20.44434pt\Gamma\vdash_{H}a \to b}{\Gamma\vdash_{H}b}
$$
where $H$ is the set of substitution instances of the axioms
\ref{it:H1} -- \ref{it:H6} above.
We write ${H}\vdash a$ if $\emptyset\vdash_{H}a$.
\par Our goal is to show that this Hilbert system is equivalent to
the equational system used to axiomatise implicative meet
semilattices in the literature (see e.g.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Nem65}{}{}]}) introduced next.
\par\defi Let ${E}$ be the equational system consisting of
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$a\wedge(b\wedge c)=(a\wedge b)\wedge c$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$a\wedge b=b\wedge a$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$a\wedge a=a$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$a\wedge\top=a$.
\end{enumerate}The set of \emph{provable} equations in ${E}$ is the least set of
equations containing all substitution instances of \ref{it:E1}
-- \ref{it:E4} above that is closed under the rules of equational
reasoning
$$(\mathrm{ref})\frac{}{a=a}\qquad(\mathrm{sym})\frac{a=b}{b=a}\qquad(\mathrm{trans})\frac{a=b\quad b=c}{a=c}\qquad(\mathrm{cong})\frac{a_{1}=b_{1}\quad a_{2}=b_{2}}{a_{1}\circ a_{2}=b_{1}\circ b_{2}}$$for $\circ\in\{\land,\to\}$, as well as the
residuation equivalence (displayed as a pair of rules)
$$\frac{a\land b\leq c}{a\leq b\to c}\qquad\frac{a\leq b\to c}{a\land b\leq c}$$where we write, as usual, $a\leq b$ for $a\land b=a$.
\par Our goal is to establish soundness and completeness of ${H}$ with
respect to ${E}$. That is, ${H}\vdash a$ iff ${E}\vdash a=\top$. We begin with a few simple facts on derivability in ${H}$,
where $\Gamma$ is a set of formulae, and $a,b$ are formulae. The
first is the admissibility of weakening.
\par\par\begin{lem}$\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}b$ whenever $\Gamma\vdash_{H}b$.
\end{lem}\par\noindent The proof is a routine induction on derivations, and therefore
omitted. The next two facts are used in the proof of the deduction
theorem.
\par\begin{lem}We have
(1) $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\to a$;
and (2) If $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a$ then $\Gamma\vdash_{H}b\to a$.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}For the second item, note that $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\to(b\to a)$ by \ref{it:H1}. As $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a$ by assumption, an
application of (mp) gives $\Gamma\vdash_{H}b\to a$.
\par For the first claim, note that we have the following three instances
of axioms
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent\begin{tabular}[]{>{(}wl{2ex}<{)}>{$}wl{8mm}<{$}>{$}wl{65mm}<{$}wr{4cm}}1&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&(a\to(a\to a)\to a)\to((a\to(a\to a))\to(a\to a))&\ref{it:H2}\\
2&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to((a\to a)\to a)&\ref{it:H1}\\
3&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to(a\to a)&\ref{it:H1}\end{tabular}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent
whence we may conclude that
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent\begin{tabular}[]{>{(}wl{2ex}<{)}>{$}wl{8mm}<{$}>{$}wl{65mm}<{$}wr{4cm}}4&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&(a\to(a\to a))\to(a\to a)&({mp} 2 1)\\
5&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to a&({mp} 3 4)\end{tabular}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent
as required.
\end{proof}\par With these preparations, we are now ready for the deduction theorem.
\par\begin{lem}We have $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\to b$ if and only if $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}b$.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}For ``only if'' assume that $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\to b$. Using
weakening (Lemma \ref{lemma:weak}) we obtain that $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}a\to b$. As $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}a$ by
(\assum), an application of ({mp}) yields the claim.
\par We show ``if'' by induction on the derivation of $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}b$.
If $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}b$ has been derived using (\assum),
we distinguish the cases $b\in\Gamma$ and $a=b$.
In the first case,
$\Gamma\vdash_{H}b$ (again using (\assum))
whence $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\to b$
using Lemma \ref{lemma:simple}. If $a=b$, then
Lemma \ref{lemma:simple} again gives $\Gamma\vdash a\to a$,
i.e.~$\Gamma\vdash a\to b$.
\par If $b\in H$ is a substitution
instance of \ref{it:H1} -- \ref{it:H6}, then $\Gamma\vdash b$
(for the same reason), and again Lemma \ref{lemma:simple} gives
$\Gamma\vdash a\to b$.
\par The final case to consider is that
$\Gamma,b$ was derived using
({mp}). This means that there is a formula $c$ and shorter
derivations of $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}c\to b$ and $\Gamma,a\vdash_{H}c$. We apply the induction hypotheses and
instantiate \ref{it:H2} to obtain
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent\begin{tabular}[]{>{(}wl{2ex}<{)}>{$}wl{8mm}<{$}>{$}wl{65mm}<{$}wr{4cm}}1&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to(c\to b)&(IH)\\
2&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to c&(IH)\\
3&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&(a\to(c\to b))\to((a\to c)\to(a\to b))&\ref{it:H2}\end{tabular}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent
which allows us to use $(\textrm{mp})$ twice to obtain that
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent\begin{tabular}[]{>{(}wl{2ex}<{)}>{$}wl{8mm}<{$}>{$}wl{65mm}<{$}wr{4cm}}4&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&(a\to c)\to(a\to b)&({mp} 1 3)\\
5&\Gamma\vdash_{H}&a\to b&({mp} 2 4)\end{tabular}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent
as required, which finishes the proof.
\end{proof}\par\par Our reasoning so far is valid in minimal logic, and we are adding
conjunction. The next lemma describes the mechanics of reasoning
with conjunction, both on the left and on the right.
\par\begin{lem}$\Gamma\vdash a\land b$ if and only if both $\Gamma\vdash a$ and
$\Gamma\vdash_{H}b$.
Also, for any formula $c$,
$\Gamma,a\land b\vdash c$ if and only if $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}c$.
\end{lem}\begin{proof}The first claim is straightforward. Note that $\Gamma\vdash a\to(b\to a\land b)$ by ($\mathrm{ax}$). Now
apply ({mp}) twice. For the converse, we have that both $\Gamma\vdash a\land b\to a$ and $\Gamma\vdash a\land b\to b$
and we obtain the claim using ({mp}). We turn to the second
statement.
\par First suppose that $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash c$. By the
deduction theorem, $\Gamma\vdash_{H}a\land b\to c$. Using
weakening this gives $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}a\land b\to c$. On the other hand, we have $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}a$ and
$\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}b$ using (\assum) so that the first part of
this lemma gives $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}a\land b$.
We then get $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}c$ from ({mp}).
\par Conversely, suppose that $\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}c$.
It is easy to see that $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}a$, as
$\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}a\land b$ by (\assum). Also,
$\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}a\land b\to a$ by ($\mathrm{ax}$) so
that applying ({mp}) gives $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash a$. A
symmetric argument shows that $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}b$.
Using the deduction theorem and weakening, we obtain $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}a\to(b\to c)$ from our assumption
$\Gamma,a,b\vdash_{H}c$. As both $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}a$ and $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}b$, applying ({mp})
twice yields $\Gamma,a\land b\vdash_{H}c$ as desired.
\end{proof}\par We now have collected all required prerequisites for the soundness
of the equational system ${E}$ with respect to ${H}$.
\begin{prop}If ${E}\vdash a=b$, then ${H}\vdash a\to b$ and ${H}\vdash b\to a$.
\end{prop}\begin{proof}The proof proceeds by induction on the derivation.
We first show that ${H}\vdash a\to b$ and ${H}\vdash b\to a$ for all substitution
instances of the equations \ref{it:E1} -- \ref{it:E4}.
\par We begin with \ref{it:E1}, i.e.~associativity. By Lemma
\ref{lemma:conj} we have that $a,b,c\vdash_{H}a\land b$,
and a second application yields $a,b,c\vdash_{H}(a\land b)\land c$. Applying the second part of the same lemma twice now gives
$a\land(b\land c)\vdash_{H}(a\land b)\land c$. A
symmetric argument shows that ${H}\vdash(a\land b)\land c\to a\land(b\land c)$.
\par For commutativity, i.e.~\ref{it:E2}, note that $a,b\vdash b\land a$ by Lemma \ref{lemma:conj} whence $a\land b\vdash_{H}b\land a$ by the same lemma. The deduction theorem
gives ${H}\vdash a\land b\to b\land a$. Swapping $a$ and
$b$ gives the converse implication.
\par We turn to \ref{it:E3}, idempotency. Note that ${H}\vdash a\land a\to a$ by ($\mathrm{ax}$). The converse direction, i.e. ${H}\vdash a\to a\land a$ follows from observing that $a\to a\land a$ is a consequence of Lemma \ref{lemma:conj} and an
application of the deduction theorem.
\par For identity, that is \ref{it:E4}, note that $a\land\top\to a$ is an instance of \ref{it:H3}. We show the converse
direction, i.e.~${H}\vdash a\to a\land\top$. As $a\vdash_{H}a$ and $a\vdash_{H}\top$ by (\assum) and ($\mathrm{ax}$),
respectively, Lemma \ref{lemma:conj} gives that $a\vdash_{H}a\land\top$, and an application of the deduction theorem yields
the claim.
\par We turn to the laws of equational logic. For ($\mathrm{ref}$), note that
${H}\vdash a\to a$ by Lemma \ref{lemma:simple}. If ${E}\vdash a=b$ is has been derived from ${E}\vdash b=a$ using
symmetry, the claim is immediate as the inductive hypothesis
yields ${H}\vdash b\to a$ and ${H}\vdash a\to b$.
\par Now assume that ${E}\vdash a_{1}\land b_{1}=a_{2}\land b_{2}$ has
been derived using congruence from ${E}\vdash a_{1}=a_{2}$ and
${E}\vdash b_{1}=b_{2}$. We only show that ${H}\vdash a_{1}\land b_{1}\to a_{2}\land b_{2}$ as the other implication is almost
identical. Applying the deduction theorem to the induction
hypotheses (the shorter derivations of $a_{1}=a_{2}$ and $b_{1}=b_{2}$) we obtain that $a_{1}\vdash_{H}a_{2}$ and $b_{1}\vdash_{H}b_{2}$. Using weakening and Lemma \ref{lemma:conj}
this gives $a_{1},b_{1}\vdash a_{2}\land b_{2}$ and applying Lemma
\ref{lemma:conj} again, together with the deduction theorem, gives
${H}\vdash a_{1}\land b_{1}\to a_{2}\land b_{2}$.
\par For the second congruence law, assume that ${E}\vdash a_{1}\to b_{1}=a_{2}\to b_{2}$ has
been derived using congruence from ${E}\vdash a_{1}=a_{2}$ and
${E}\vdash b_{1}=b_{2}$. Again, we only demonstrate that ${H}\vdash(a_{1}\to b_{1})\to(a_{2}\to b_{2})$. The induction
hypotheses for the derivations of $a_{1}=a_{2}$ and $b_{1}=b_{2}$
imply
${H}\vdash a_{2}\to a_{1}$ and ${H}\vdash b_{1}\to b_{2}$,
respectively. We obtain:
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent\begin{tabular}[]{>{(}wl{2ex}<{)}>{$}wl{24mm}<{$}>{$}wl{65mm}<{$}wr{4cm}}1&a_{1}\to b_{1},a_{2}\vdash_{H}&a_{2}\to a_{1}&(IH) and weakening\\
2&a_{1}\to b_{1},a_{2}\vdash_{H}&a_{1}&\end{tabular}
